<a id="prologue"></a>

# Prologue

## The other life of a song

In one version of a small online music market, a song called Lockdown finished first. In another, it finished fortieth out of forty-eight. The band was called 52Metro. The recording competing for attention was the same.[^prologue-1]

First place invites a story. The band understood its audience. The song had something the others lacked. Listen again, knowing it won, and you might hear the qualities that explain its success. Fortieth invites a different story. The song failed to connect. Other bands understood something 52Metro did not. Listen again, knowing it lost, and its shortcomings might become obvious.

Usually, we get to tell only one of those stories. Someone succeeds, and the rest of us work backward. We examine the product, the strategy, the years of effort. The ending helps us decide which details mattered. We rarely get to check our explanation against another history in which the same work met a different fate.

For Lockdown, someone had kept the other chart.

MusicLab was a website where people could discover unfamiliar music. In research published in 2006, Matthew Salganik, Peter Dodds, and Duncan Watts studied choices made by 14,341 participants across two experiments involving forty-eight songs. Some listeners saw no download totals. Others could see what earlier listeners had downloaded. These listeners were randomly assigned to eight separate worlds, each with its own evolving popularity record. Every song began with zero downloads.[^prologue-2]

A download in one world did nothing to the chart in another. Different groups of listeners encountered the same recordings, but accumulated different histories. In one experiment, popularity appeared as numbers beside songs in a grid. In the other, the socially influenced listeners saw a list ranked by popularity. Earlier choices could help determine what the next person noticed.[^prologue-1]

The stronger the social influence, the more unequal and unpredictable success became across worlds. Appeal still mattered. Songs that attracted listeners without popularity information rarely went from the very best to the very worst, or vice versa. But appeal did not dictate the finishing order.[^prologue-2]

Lockdown had acquired something more revealing than a good review or a bad one: another result. Its first-place finish was real. So was its fortieth-place finish. Any convincing account of its success now had to leave room for both.

The same question turned up a few years later among projects asking for real money.

On Kickstarter, researchers led by Arnout van de Rijt selected 200 new, unfunded projects. They gave an initial donation to 100 chosen at random. The first backer, whose presence later visitors could see, had picked a project by chance. The researchers then watched who else would put money behind it.[^prologue-3]

Among projects that received no experimental donation, 39 percent subsequently attracted money from other donors. Among those given the initial donation, 70 percent did. That later support excluded the researchers' own contribution. The first donation had helped bring others behind it.[^prologue-3]

An observer arriving afterward would see projects with several backers and projects with none. The successful pitches might seem clearer, their ambitions more compelling. Without knowing about the random donation, the observer could give the pitch credit for an advantage assigned by an experiment.

The researchers also tested larger initial advantages. Their effects showed diminishing returns: more manufactured success did not produce proportionately more subsequent success.[^prologue-3] An initial boost mattered, but increasing it did not keep buying the same additional response.

People can respond sensibly to a record of success while helping create the next success in that record. The later backers' money is real. The project may put it to excellent use. Yet counting those backers does not tell us how much support the project would have received without its experimental head start.

Return to Lockdown. Add another thousand listeners to the world where it finished first. We might learn much more about that audience's enjoyment. Those additional listeners would not recreate the world where the song finished fortieth. More observations of one history and more chances to compare histories are different things.

Markets do more than distribute money and attention. They help determine which alternatives get tried, which improve through use, and which produce a record anyone can inspect. A market can become very busy while leaving a particular question poorly answered: how much did productive work, rather than the path of earlier rewards, contribute to this success?

I call the cumulative chance of rewards going somewhere other than the current favorite the comparison budget. At each opportunity, take the chance that someone else receives the reward, then add those chances across opportunities. The favorite can change. This counts chances, rather than actual rival victories; it does not make every purchase or download an independent experiment. The question is how much opportunity the allocation process leaves for informative comparison.

MusicLab makes the distinction tangible. Forty-eight song titles can remain on a screen even while attention flows mainly toward a few. Keeping the titles available is different from getting the recordings heard. A long list of alternatives can coexist with a thin record of what they can do.

To investigate this question, I developed models of how markets distribute rewards. In these models, the comparison budget limits how precisely a reward history can reveal the effects of work, quality, and investment. Under specified conditions, opportunities outside the favorite can dwindle fast enough that even an indefinitely growing record cannot reliably settle attribution. A small chance that persists can still accumulate. The way those chances change over time matters, alongside their size today.

Success breeding success is an old idea. Robert Merton described how recognition could bring scientists resources for further work. Armen Alchian explained market selection under uncertainty decades before MusicLab.[^prologue-5] A lead can keep growing while opportunities to check its causes disappear. The growing reward and the shrinking capacity to explain it can be consequences of the same process.

Lockdown's other chart suggests what is missing. I call these feasible alternative histories shadow futures, borrowing words from Steven Johnson's account of Stuart Kauffman's adjacent possible.[^prologue-10] They share the same underlying economic conditions and rules, but early events differ. Later choices and effort can respond. The recording stays the same; different first listeners leave a different chart for everyone afterward.

Sometimes policy changes which alternatives can become real. In 1956, an American antitrust settlement opened 7,820 existing Bell System patents to royalty-free licensing. Bell Laboratories had helped create technologies including the transistor. Other businesses now had access to thousands of patented inventions without paying royalties.[^prologue-6]

Decades later, Martin Watzinger and his colleagues studied the consequences. Comparing Bell patents with matched patents outside Bell, they found a lasting increase in subsequent innovation outside telecommunications. They did not find the same increase within telecommunications, where access to customers remained obstructed. Permission to use an invention was not always enough to make a business from it.[^prologue-6]

The result gives the missing comparison a physical form: equipment that another company might build, offer, and improve. An invention behind a licensing barrier cannot tell us everything about what other inventors could do with it. Opening access changes the rules; it is an intervention, rather than a replay of an unchanged economy. But it can let possibilities previously confined to speculation develop observable results.

A restriction can prevent useful discovery before it raises a price. A rival may lose its route to a first customer, leaving no finished product for buyers to compare. Preserving that route can matter before there is a price dispute. Its value depends on what another supplier could test and what people could learn, as well as the costs of making the trial possible.

Preserving that route becomes more urgent when the thing being bought can help decide who gets hired, what work gets done, and which ideas receive attention.

Consider a customer-support worker with an AI assistant suggesting replies during a conversation. The worker can accept, edit, or ignore them. This was the setting studied by Erik Brynjolfsson, Danielle Li, and Lindsey Raymond, using data from 5,172 agents. Their published study found an average increase of 15 percent in issues resolved per hour after access to the assistant, with especially strong improvements among less experienced and lower-skilled workers.[^prologue-7]

Here is a reason to welcome the technology. A newer worker can draw on assistance that helps close a gap in experience. The study also found evidence of learning. AI can make useful knowledge more available to people who have had less time to acquire it. Any account of the future of work has to make room for gains like these.

The researchers examined periods when the AI system went offline and agents continued handling their customer conversations. Workers who had used the assistant still handled conversations faster than they had before gaining access. The pattern was stronger among people with more previous exposure to its suggestions. This was evidence consistent with learning: some benefit could remain when the immediate stream of advice stopped. A worker's improved performance could therefore reflect both what the software supplied now and something the worker had acquired through using it.[^prologue-7]

That interruption gives the comparison a concrete purpose. Watching a successful assisted conversation tells us what worker and tool accomplished together. Watching work continue during an outage helps investigate what the worker can do when suggestions disappear. Neither record alone describes every possible workplace. Together, they let us ask a sharper question about how a productive ability develops. The distinction matters when an employer decides whether software should guide beginners, handle their tasks itself, or change the training through which the next generation learns the job.

But a customer-support result cannot settle what happens in a software project, any more than Lockdown's winning chart can settle its fortunes everywhere. The work, tools, and opportunities to measure them differ. To see how quickly the comparison itself can change, follow what happened to researchers studying programmers.

METR, an organization that evaluates AI systems, ran an experiment in early 2025 in which experienced developers completed tasks with or without AI tools. In that setting, AI access made completion slower. When METR expanded the research later that year, it encountered a different problem: some developers did not want to participate if participation meant doing work without AI.[^prologue-8]

They were being offered fifty dollars an hour to work on their own projects. Still, some declined. Others held tasks back from random assignment because they did not want those tasks landing in the condition where AI was forbidden. In surveys, between 30 and 50 percent of developers reported withholding some tasks for that reason.[^prologue-8]

By February 2026, METR said the newer results gave an unreliable measure of the productivity effect. Changed pay and difficulties measuring time also complicated matters. Its researchers thought AI was likely helping developers more than before, while emphasizing how weakly their data measured the size of that improvement.[^prologue-8]

Think of a task the programmer keeps out of the study. It may be exactly the task on which AI saves the most time. The developer has a reason to keep using the tool, while the researcher has a reason to want the missing comparison. Both can be acting sensibly. The work gets done, but that opportunity to measure the difference has gone.

METR began redesigning its experiments. When developers change how they work, researchers have to change how they measure it. The question is how to preserve useful comparisons without asking people to abandon the tools they now rely on.

The same practical question runs from the programmer's desk to the data center. An artificial intelligence (AI) model is a computer system that uses patterns learned from data to make predictions or generate material such as text. Training an AI model means adjusting its internal settings using examples to improve its performance at a task, such as predicting the next piece of text. Running the trained model to produce an answer is called inference. Training, inference, and comparing one model with another are different activities.

A service can generate an enormous record of answers while leaving unanswered whether a different model, supplier, or organization of work would serve us better. AI can also make those comparisons cheaper. Which possibility prevails depends on how we deploy it.

The consequences reach the pay packet. Think again of the customer-support worker resolving more problems with AI assistance. The worker is doing useful work. So are the people who built the tool. The employer supplies an organization in which both can operate. Measuring the productivity improvement helps us understand what changed. It does not, on its own, tell us how to divide the additional income among them.

Now remember the Kickstarter projects. Their later support was influenced by a donation the researchers deliberately assigned. In ordinary business, we rarely have such a clearly marked intervention. We see the revenues after customers, reputation, investment, and opportunity have acted on one another. That makes the familiar claim that someone deserves precisely what the market paid them much harder to establish than the claim that they created something valuable.

Taxation enters here because governments must choose rules even when contribution cannot be divided neatly from position. Incentives matter; promises and legitimate entitlements matter. Uncertainty about a fortune's origins supplies no tax rate. It does require us to explain our tax rules without pretending that every pound or dollar arrives with a reliable account of who caused it.

When closing a route to customers prevents other people from learning something useful, part of the cost may fall outside the business making that choice. Access rules, public trials, or a carefully justified tax could address that loss. Their justification depends on what people have lost the chance to learn, how it affects their decisions, and whether the remedy is worth its costs.

We already know how to treat some comparisons as worth maintaining. At Rothamsted in England, agricultural experiments begun in 1843 have preserved differences in how plots are treated across generations. Soil and plant samples have been kept for more than 180 years. Some plots have received particular fertilizer or manure treatments since the beginning, apart from fallow periods. Methods have also changed to keep the experiments useful.[^prologue-9]

Those fields are productive in two senses. They grow crops, and their maintained differences help produce knowledge. Maximizing this year's harvest on every plot would answer a different question from the one the continuing experiments make possible. Someone has to value the comparison enough to keep it alive.

A song, a first donation, a patent, an AI assistant: each produces something people can value. Each also changes what happens next. The economy's rewards help decide which possibilities become familiar and which remain out of sight.

Lockdown finished first. Lockdown finished fortieth. The second chart survived because somebody built a place for it. The harder question is what happens to the futures for which we never make room.

<a id="introduction"></a>

# Introduction

## The evidence success leaves behind

### The lives I did not live

I have spent an unreasonable amount of time thinking about the lives I did not live.

I studied economics at the University of Victoria in Canada and took a route through door-to-door sales, landscaping, and software development. I have worked on cloud infrastructure and deployments. I never pursued an academic career. In several of those worlds I felt that I had arrived by the wrong entrance and never quite belonged.

There are choices I still resent having made. A different investment, a business begun earlier, a career pursued more consistently: hindsight makes it easy to furnish another life. It supplies the outcome I want and leaves out the decisions I would have faced along the way.

In computing, simulated annealing lets a search move away from its current solution in pursuit of a better one. I have done rather too much of the personal equivalent. Changing direction can reveal something worth finding. It can also keep you from building on what you have found.

My regrets kept returning me to a larger question. I could remember some of the opportunities I had missed. What about the opportunities that never became available to anyone? How could a market learn about a business that nobody could get started?

### The advertisement that got the credit

A customer searches for eBay, clicks an advertisement for eBay, and buys something on eBay. For the advertiser, the journey looks reassuringly complete. There was an ad, then a click, then a sale. Each step can be recorded. Money went out to attract the customer; money came back when the customer bought. Here, apparently, is an unusually clean account of a contribution.

Except that the customer was searching for eBay already.

Economists Thomas Blake, Chris Nosko, and Steven Tadelis studied large advertising experiments at the company. When advertising against its own brand terms was suspended, customers largely substituted unpaid search links for paid ones. The brand advertisements had no measurable short-term benefit. Experiments with more general search terms found a different pattern: new and infrequent customers could be influenced, while frequent customers accounted for much spending without a corresponding change in purchases.[^introduction-i1]

One brand-keyword test stopped eBay advertising on Yahoo and Microsoft's search platform while retaining Google advertising as a comparison. Almost all the lost paid-search traffic and attributed sales reappeared through unpaid links. Customers had another route to the shop. Closing the paid route revealed how many sales the advertising ledger had mistakenly credited.[^introduction-i1]

The advertisements existed. People designed them, placed them, and paid for them. Some helped customers. Counting that activity did not establish how much it changed purchases.

For the shopper who wanted eBay, the alternative was close at hand: another link on a search page. In other settings, the alternative is much harder to recover. It might be another business that never attracted a first customer, another worker who never received an assignment, or another technology that never developed a record of successful use. We can study what happened in great detail while remaining uncertain about what those alternatives would have revealed.

Success can close the comparisons needed to explain success. An early reward changes who gets the next serious opportunity, and therefore what later observers can learn. The economy produces goods and services while selecting which alternatives acquire a history. More transactions can deepen the record of a winner without settling how much its productive work caused the lead.

To measure the advertisement's short-term contribution, eBay needed customers who could arrive without it. Recording the paid clicks more accurately would not recreate that comparison.

### How success changes the evidence

Economists and sociologists have long studied how success builds on itself. Armen Alchian explained how profits select surviving firms without requiring perfect foresight. Robert Merton described how recognition brings scientists resources for further work. W. Brian Arthur showed how increasing returns can let early adoption influence which technology becomes established.[^introduction-i6]

Network science offers another familiar mechanism. In preferential attachment, a web page that already has many links is more likely to attract another link. Albert-László Barabási and Réka Albert combined that process with network growth to explain a power-law distribution of links, with a small number of highly connected hubs.[^introduction-i13] Growth, lower costs, and unequal outcomes can accompany either plentiful comparisons or very few. Explaining a winner's contribution requires evidence about the opportunities that actually remain to test alternatives.

Consider a platform that sends more tasks to workers with longer records. The feedback can widen their lead and get useful work done. It also determines what anyone can learn about workers who rarely receive a task. A thousand completed jobs for the favorite might reveal little about someone never hired. The problem concerns the production of evidence, before any judgment about whether the winner deserves the income.

### The work and the difference it made

I developed this question in a research paper, *Shadow Futures: Contribution Uncertainty and the Self-Reinforcing Market*, available on SSRN. It introduces the comparison budget and shows how opportunities outside the favorite constrain what an allocation record can reveal about productive contribution.[^introduction-i2]

I use contribution uncertainty for uncertainty about the effect of productive inputs on rewards in a specified economic setting. The inputs might include effort, quality, investment, or risk-bearing. The question concerns how an outcome would change if an input changed, while the relevant economic rules continued to apply. A result can be valuable, and the work behind it entirely real, while the size of that effect remains difficult to recover.[^introduction-i2]

Visits, purchases, and longer-term customer relationships are different outcomes. Contribution concerns a specified outcome, comparison, and time horizon.

Lockdown's two rankings raise a related question with a different design. MusicLab preserved independently developing markets for the same songs. The song's first-place finish and its fortieth-place finish were both outcomes of listeners choosing. They tell us that one observed ranking cannot be treated as a complete account of what that recording was capable of achieving. They do not calculate the value of every rehearsal or assign a percentage of the song's success to talent.[^introduction-i3]

A recording can give pleasure in several possible histories and receive very different amounts of attention in each. The pleasure is real. How much of the reward it explains remains a question about causes.

In George Akerlof's account of the used-car market, the seller may know more about the car's quality than the buyer does. A buyer unable to distinguish good cars from bad ones may offer a price that drives good cars away. The information problem changes which trades take place.[^introduction-i4]

My starting question allows the inputs to be observed. Suppose the buyer can inspect the work, rather than suspecting a hidden defect. That still leaves the effect of those inputs on the outcome to be learned. Knowing how much an advertising campaign cost does not identify the sales it created. Knowing what a musician recorded does not reveal every audience the recording could have found. Better disclosure and better comparison can solve different problems.

Kickstarter's experimental first donations helped some projects attract subsequent backing. In an ordinary business, an early customer can also finance better engineering, more practice, and a stronger organization. The advantage becomes productive when it improves what later customers receive. Asking whether the business is good and asking how it became good are separate inquiries.[^introduction-i5]

### Who gets a chance

Each choice does two things: it allocates resources now and helps determine what the next observer will be able to inspect. An expanding record of success can therefore coexist with fewer opportunities to evaluate alternatives.

For each allocation, given the history so far, identify the candidate with the greatest chance of receiving the reward. Take the chance that someone else receives it instead. Accumulate those chances across the opportunities under study. I call this economic quantity the comparison budget. The favorite can change. The total concerns the chances offered to alternatives, rather than the number of times an outsider actually wins.[^introduction-i2]

A list of awards and rejections does not, by itself, tell us those chances. The budget is not a count of winners or the number of firms on a website. To apply the idea empirically, we need evidence about the allocation process: which alternatives are eligible, what changes their prospects, and what is recorded beyond the award. The quantities cannot be read straight from market shares.

MusicLab helps us see why a catalogue is insufficient. A title remaining on a list does not mean listeners encounter it on comparable terms. Conversely, a single business can arrange useful tests of alternatives, as eBay did with advertising. Counting organizations and counting informative opportunities are different inquiries. Institutional design can matter as much as the number of logos on the screen.

Line Chloé Le Goff and Philippe Soulier had already connected reinforcement to estimation failure in urn models.[^introduction-i14] The comparison budget connects the remaining allocation chances to a limit on information about productive input effects. In my models it bounds how much information the reward record supplies. Under the additional conditions developed in Chapter 3, a finite lifetime budget prevents any method from reliably recovering contribution across every explanation the model allows. Independent measurements change the available evidence.[^introduction-i2]

A tiny but persistent chance of selecting an outsider can keep accumulating. A shrinking chance can also accumulate without limit if it shrinks slowly enough. Even then, comparisons must distinguish the input effects we care about. A trial that never varies the relevant engineering tells us little about its contribution, however often it runs.

Another transaction can demonstrate that an established service continues to work well while revealing little about an untested alternative. Commercial value and information about a particular comparison are separate properties of the same event.

Field experiments, independent technical evaluations, and measured outcomes can distinguish contributions that an award record leaves uncertain. Their information adds to what can be learned.

eBay could change where advertising appeared and observe what followed. Its advertising ledger did not have to remain the last word. A feasible intervention could supply the comparison that the existing record lacked.

### Other possible histories

Melanie Mitchell, Douglas Hofstadter, and M. Mitchell Waldrop first drew me into complexity through their popular writing. I went on to read W. Brian Arthur, Stuart Kauffman, Eric Beinhocker, and John Holland, alongside economists such as Kenneth Arrow. I even traveled to Washington, D.C., for a Santa Fe Institute short course. Economics had become a way of asking how a world makes things that nobody planned.

There was something almost spiritual in the thought that the world held possibilities no one had yet imagined. There was something unforgiving about it too. We would live only some of them.

In September 2016, I published a Medium essay called “Increasing Returns and the New World of Hindsight.” I was already asking how a company's growth could strengthen its confidence in a history that left its alternatives unseen.[^introduction-i19] The comparison budget came later. The question had been following me for years.

The seed of this book's name was a phrase in Steven Johnson's account of Stuart A. Kauffman's adjacent possible. Kauffman, author of *At Home in the Universe*, explored self-organization and complexity. The adjacent possible describes possibilities reachable from what already exists. Johnson called it “a kind of shadow future.” Existing materials and discoveries put some next steps within reach; taking them can make further combinations possible.[^introduction-i12]

Think of Bell's transistor. An invention can become a component in devices its inventors never built. Who can work with it matters to which possibilities are explored. Johnson's image made me ask an economic question: what happens to possibilities that depend on opportunities going to someone who never gets them?

I chose *shadow futures* because the phrase holds together two views of the same opportunity. Before a customer places an order, more than one business may have a future that could grow from it. Afterward, we can follow the business that won: the revenue it receives, the investment it makes, the work it learns to do. The other path recedes from view. It may never leave enough of a record for us to judge what could have developed there.

I use the term for feasible alternative allocation histories with the same starting conditions, underlying productive effects, and rules for how people respond. The first customer can change; so can the later investment financed by that first sale. The rules stay fixed while events unfold differently. Kauffman's adjacent possible concerns the opening of new possibilities. My question concerns what we can learn when only one of the available paths becomes observable.

MusicLab makes the idea visible. Lockdown could finish first or fortieth without becoming a different recording. The researchers kept the other chart. An ordinary business rarely gets an equivalent record of the opportunities it missed ten years ago.

That is why I chose a phrase about unrealized futures for a result about knowledge. A path we do not observe can contain a comparison we need to explain the one we do. Another order for the winner adds to its history. It need not tell us what the rival would have learned, built, or earned if an earlier order had gone the other way.

This book is itself a product of the adjacent possible. Those writers gave me questions I could carry into jobs and ventures they knew nothing about. Its future will depend on who encounters it and what they can do with it. A professional economist may give these ideas a larger audience, sooner than I can. Credentials, readers, and access to the right conversations help an idea find people able to develop it.

If someone else tests the idea more rigorously, explains it better, or helps more people use it, I will be content with that outcome. Credit matters, as does the freedom to challenge a claim. What I do not want is an epistemic monopoly over an idea that could benefit humanity. I want the comparison to remain open, including when an alternative to my account proves better. I cannot ask readers to take shadow futures seriously and then assume that the history giving me the most recognition must be the best one.

Changing an early award within unchanged rules explores another history. Changing the rules is an intervention. eBay altered an arrangement and measured the response. A simulated alteration would also need evidence that its programmed responses fit the actual market.

Predicting what comes next and explaining why the outcome has the shape it does answer different questions. In Lockdown's winning world, an observer who expects more listeners to choose an already popular song could make useful forecasts. Those forecasts would not reveal the other world's chart. Getting the next choice right does not itself account for the song's lead.

Blackwell and Dubins showed that, under specified conditions, observers' forecasts converge as evidence accumulates. Hao Zhang later demonstrated a statistical setting in which useful prediction coexists with failure to recover particular underlying parameters. Prediction and explanation can demand different things from the same record.[^introduction-i15]

An early research grant makes the stakes concrete. Thijs Bol, Mathijs de Vaan, and Arnout van de Rijt studied Dutch scientists whose applications fell just above or below a funding threshold. The near-winners subsequently accumulated more than twice as much further funding over eight years as the near-losers, excluding the initial grant itself. Part of the gap arose because unsuccessful applicants entered later funding competitions less often. The distinction involved who continued to present work for evaluation, as well as how evaluators treated it.[^introduction-i11]

The research proposal that never reaches another panel leaves a different kind of absence from the one a rejected proposal leaves. We can inspect the latter decision. The former requires asking which earlier events affected participation. When opportunity influences the decision to compete again, a record of later funding awards can conceal part of the process producing its own pool of candidates. That is a reason to follow the people outside the winner's history, even when the published results look complete.

### From evidence to institutions

At the customer-support desk studied by Brynjolfsson, Li, and Raymond, AI assistance helped workers resolve more issues per hour on average. The gains were particularly strong among less experienced and lower-skilled workers. Useful capability was reaching people who could benefit from it.[^introduction-i7]

Training future workers raises a further question. Today's assistant might help beginners learn, or do the tasks through which they would have learned. Comparing their later abilities requires following what they attempt, the guidance they receive, and what they can do afterward.

METR's developer research exposes one difficulty. As programmers became less willing to do certain work without AI, the research team found that participation and task selection made its follow-up estimates difficult to interpret. The work being studied was changing which comparisons people would supply. The team responded by reconsidering its design. Learning about the technology required attention to the institutions producing evidence about it.[^introduction-i8]

The reach of a judgment changes the stakes. Smith's artisan faced a market limited by transport; another town could offer another buyer. A platform can carry one rating across towns, while a shared infrastructure provider can control the route used by many apparently separate businesses. Physical limits persist, and old guilds also obstructed entry. What changes when a common gate spans the market is how far an exclusion travels.[^introduction-i16]

More computing can make experiments cheaper while routing more work through the same model, service, or evaluator. Where one institution controls the comparisons others need to judge alternatives, it holds an epistemic monopoly over those comparisons.[^introduction-i17] Productive scale and control over discovery can grow together.

Bell's 1956 settlement provides a historical warning against treating access as a slogan. Opening existing patents to royalty-free licensing was followed by greater innovation outside telecommunications. The study did not find the same result inside the sector, where barriers to reaching customers remained. A policy can loosen one constraint while leaving another in place. An entrant needs a usable route from permission to performance.[^introduction-i9]

Lost comparison can impose a cost that never appears on the customer's bill. A gatekeeper may improve its service while closing a route others need to develop or test theirs. When that closure deprives other people of valuable evidence, part of its cost falls outside the gatekeeper's decision. Many buyers can also favor the same route without one supplier controlling the whole market. Their transactions record the work completed; the missing trial leaves no failed product to count.

For such a market, waiting for a price increase can mean waiting until an alternative has lost its feasible beginning. Existing competition policy considers innovation and exclusion as well as prices.[^introduction-i18] Protecting a workable route to independent evaluation gives those concerns an earlier point of action. Credible threats of lasting closure should trigger a presumption for keeping that route open, with security, investment, and the costs of access included in its terms.

For taxation, explaining an income and changing future incentives are different tasks. Conduct that prevents useful comparison can impose costs on others, justifying a corrective charge where those costs are established. Shared research, broader ownership, and a universal basic income can give the public a stake in the returns while preserving the capacity to begin again. Their case need not depend on proving that particular recipients were morally undeserving.

Consider a capable team that cannot afford its first credible trial while existing owners use their returns to consolidate the routes to customers. Leaving both positions untouched is a choice about the next opportunity. Using part of capital income and large inheritances to finance shared research and broader access to assets is another. The second can support an economy with room for new judgments and new owners. Its tax rates and spending choices must be assessed through investment, avoidance, administration, and the benefits people actually receive.

An independent test, access to existing records, and a change in market rules produce different evidence at different costs. A useful remedy improves a consequential decision enough to justify those costs. Sometimes the evidence will favor keeping the existing arrangement.

### A different account of success

At Rothamsted, continuing agricultural experiments make the value of maintained comparison unusually tangible. Different treatments have been preserved while methods have also been adapted to keep the experiments relevant. The record matters because someone has continued the arrangements that produce it. Useful knowledge has a material history: plots, treatments, samples, labor, and decisions about which differences to keep.[^introduction-i10]

A first customer can finance a better product. A patent license can open a field of work. A distribution agreement can close it again. These decisions help determine whose ability gets demonstrated, how capital accumulates, and what competition policy and taxation can change.

A complete record of the eBay customer's paid click could have encouraged a confident story about the advertisement. Making another route observable changed the explanation. In more consequential settings, the missing route may be a business that never reaches a customer. Its absence from the record is precisely what makes it easy to overlook.

<a id="chapter_01"></a>

# Chapter 1

## The winner and the explanation

### An ending looking for a beginning

Put Lockdown at the top of the chart and imagine that the other chart has disappeared. The first thing we know is that listeners selected it. The next thing we may want to know is what made them do so. We could listen for a memorable passage, compare the song with its competitors, and form a view about the audience. A good explanation might begin with something that really is in the recording. The trouble starts when we decide that the ranking has told us how much that feature mattered.

Lockdown's fortieth-place finish exposes the gap between a recording's appeal and its rank. Appeal could help the song in both worlds. The different rankings also depended on the sequence of attention, choices, and displayed popularity it met in each one.[^chapter_01-c1-1]

Follow a MusicLab listener. The website offered unfamiliar songs by unfamiliar bands. A participant chose a recording, listened, and was asked to rate it on a scale from one star to five. Afterward came an opportunity to download it. Downloading was optional. The experiment recorded several steps between a title appearing on a screen and a song acquiring another mark of popularity.[^chapter_01-c1-1]

A rating and a download answered different questions. The rating expressed a response after hearing the recording; in the social-influence worlds, the download entered the popularity record shown to subsequent listeners. Imagine someone who liked a track but chose not to keep it. That person's favorable judgment would not become another download on the chart. Even this simple market contained more than one way of being successful. The researchers had to choose which outcome to explain.

Now change the screen. In the first experiment, songs appeared in a grid, with positions assigned randomly for each participant. Some listeners also saw download counts. In the second, socially influenced listeners saw a single column ordered by popularity. An early download could now help move a title up the list encountered by the next listener. The recording had stayed fixed while its route to an audience changed.[^chapter_01-c1-1]

Think of a title near the bottom. To attract a listener, it has to be noticed among alternatives carrying more evidence of earlier attention. Once heard, it still has to win a download. A final download total combines those stages. A musician interested in reaching listeners would want to know where the obstacle lay: getting the title seen, getting the song played, or getting someone to keep it. The total alone cannot separate them.

Looking first at popular titles can make sense. A listener with ten spare minutes has a practical search problem. Earlier downloads may offer a useful shortcut. But that shortcut helps decide which recordings receive the next hearing. The chart both reports choices and influences the conditions under which further choices occur. It cannot be read as forty-eight recordings repeatedly taking the same unchanged test.

For Lockdown, separate worlds let the researchers investigate what an ordinary chart conceals. Each world began without a download advantage, then acquired its own record. An admirer hearing the first-place song has evidence about an encounter with that recording. The fortieth-place world adds evidence about the process through which encounters accumulate. Enjoying the song answers a real question. Explaining its finishing position requires the wider record that the experiment preserved.

Ordinary economic success comes with a similar invitation. We see the company that reached customers, the worker who developed a reputation, or the project that secured its backing. We can inspect choices those winners made. What is harder to inspect is how another beginning might have changed the circumstances under which the same choices were available or effective. That is why evidence about the beginning deserves attention before we start reading the ending as a verdict.

### Giving success away

The Kickstarter researchers did something direct about beginnings. They intervened before selected projects had raised any money. The projects already had proposals, ambitions, and creators. The researchers did not change those proposals. They changed whether an early visitor encountered a project with a first donation or a project still waiting for one. This allowed later support to be compared with a difference in initial success deliberately introduced by the research design.[^chapter_01-c1-2]

Improving a pitch and adding its first backer could both increase subsequent funding. Without the experimental assignment, an observer might mistake the second mechanism for the first. A creator could offer a polished explanation of why a successful pitch worked, and much of that explanation might be sensible. It would still need to account for the advantage that came from already having support.

The experiment found that projects given an initial donation were more likely to attract subsequent backing. Later support was counted separately from the researchers' donation. It was not enough to announce that the treated projects finished with more money, since the researchers had put money into them. The revealing result concerned what other people did afterward. The design separated the initial gift from the additional support that followed it.

That gives us a useful discipline for other claims about success. When someone says an investment brought a return, ask which part of the return has been counted. A first customer supplies revenue directly and may help attract the next customer. Those are different benefits. If we count the initial payment as evidence of the power to attract later payments, we have smuggled the intervention into the outcome. The Kickstarter design avoided that elementary trap.

The team did not stop at crowdfunding. Its experiments also intervened in product-review ratings, Wikipedia awards, and petition signatures. For Wikipedia, the subjects were highly productive editors; a randomly selected subset received a community award. The researchers then observed further awards from other people. Recognition could influence subsequent recognition even among people who were already contributing substantial work.[^chapter_01-c1-2]

The editor is a particularly instructive figure. The work exists before the experimental award. A later observer who sees several honors can reasonably think the editor has done useful things. That may be entirely correct. What the number of honors cannot establish unaided is how much each later recognition reflects the work and how much reflects the fact that the editor has already been recognized. Genuine contribution and a feedback effect can occupy the same page.

There is also no need to suppose that every later donor or award-giver merely copies a number. Early support can invite closer inspection. Closer inspection can reveal something worth supporting. An institution may benefit from such shortcuts when nobody can investigate every candidate. The experimental result concerns the consequence of receiving the early advantage. Determining the precise channel, and whether it improves decisions, requires further evidence rather than an assumption that influence is automatically foolish.

The researchers' larger interventions supply a useful warning against a seductive sequel. They compared smaller and larger manufactured advantages and found diminishing marginal returns. In the funding treatment, moving from one experimental donor to four did not create a proportionate multiplication of later success. A system can amplify the first step and respond much less strongly to the next few. The force of feedback is a question about its rules and setting.[^chapter_01-c1-2]

That result prevents us from taking the favorite story about a snowball and making it the entire theory. Some early differences persist. Some run out of force. Some are overcome by other differences. If every unequal result were offered as evidence of irresistible cumulative advantage, nothing could count against the idea. The experiments are useful precisely because they can show both amplification and its limits.

For a project creator, the practical difference might be between improving the proposal and finding a way to secure credible initial backing. Both could deserve effort. An observer trying to learn which bottleneck matters should not treat the final funding total as if it had already performed that diagnosis. The first donor has changed the situation in which the next donor decides. Understanding the record means following that change through time.

### The extra vote

A similar problem can begin with something smaller than a donation. In an experiment published in 2013, Lev Muchnik, Sinan Aral, and Sean Taylor randomly gave online comments an initial positive vote, a negative vote, or no vote. Over five months the study covered 101,281 comments on a social news website. The manipulation changed a visible score attached to words that had already been written.[^chapter_01-c1-3]

An artificial positive vote increased the likelihood that the next viewer would vote positively by 32 percent relative to the control group. Final mean scores were 25 percent higher. The percentages describe relative differences in those outcomes; they do not mean that a third of readers had been converted from disagreement to agreement. One extra mark on the screen had changed the subsequent path of evaluation.[^chapter_01-c1-3]

Here the productive object is unusually easy to hold in view. The comment did not become better written when the researchers clicked the positive button. A later reader could still find a good argument in it. But the experimental advantage was not an improvement in the argument. If a final score were used as a precise measure of the quality of the words, some of the effect of the initial vote would be assigned to the writing.

The study's site did not order comments by their popularity. That is a useful detail because it stops us from explaining the whole result as the familiar consequence of being moved higher on a ranked page. The displayed score was tied to the comment when people evaluated it. Social information could affect the subsequent rating even without that particular route through a popularity ranking.[^chapter_01-c1-3]

A comment score can help a reader find something useful while also reflecting the votes earlier readers happened to see. Its convenience and its reliability as a measure of quality depend on different features of the rating process.

Negative votes behaved differently. Readers tended to correct the artificial negative treatment, leaving final mean ratings without a statistically detectable difference from controls. The upward and downward nudges were not symmetrical.[^chapter_01-c1-3]

The topic mattered too. The researchers detected positive herding in politics, culture and society, and business. They did not detect it in economics, information technology, fun, or general news. Those categories had comparable activity levels. The same button and the same visible score did not produce a uniform response across the site.[^chapter_01-c1-3]

Compare the comment with Lockdown. In both settings, someone encounters an object together with information about earlier responses. The object may be appealing. The earlier response may also matter. The observer who sees only the accumulated result faces a mixture of those influences. The two experiments make the mixture easier to study by introducing or separating the relevant histories.

What they establish is specific enough to be useful. An early signal can affect subsequent judgment. It need not determine every outcome, work equally in both directions, or leave the underlying work unchanged in a longer economic setting. We have evidence for a mechanism and reasons to investigate its strength. We do not yet have a general permission to read all success as accidental or all rankings as worthless.

### Recognition becomes an opportunity

In his account of scientific recognition, Robert Merton used the image of a forty-first chair. The French Academy had forty places, but the history of French thought contained distinguished people who never occupied one. Merton's examples included Descartes, Molière, and Proust. An institution with a fixed number of honors cannot turn the boundary between its members and everyone else into a complete ranking of achievement.[^chapter_01-c1-4]

There are forty available chairs. An outstanding person can remain outside without anyone needing to conclude that the work was poor. A prize can be prestigious because its recipients are excellent, while failing to include everyone of comparable excellence. The quality of the winners and the completeness of the distinction are separate properties.

Merton went further. Recognition could become a resource for future research. Eminent scientists could receive disproportionate credit for contributions, and credit could help bring facilities for further work. His argument drew in part on Harriet Zuckerman's interviews with Nobel laureates. The honor was both an account of earlier achievement and a possible influence on what the scientist could do next.[^chapter_01-c1-4]

That second role changes the economic question. An extra chair would alter an honor roll. Access to a laboratory can alter the work that later appears on the roll. If we inspect achievement only after the opportunities have diverged, we may find a real difference in productive capacity. The difference can be an outcome of the earlier allocation as well as a reason for the later one.

The Dutch grant study introduced earlier makes this process less dependent on a striking phrase. Bol, de Vaan, and van de Rijt examined applicants close to a funding threshold. Those just above it later accumulated more than twice as much additional grant money over eight years as those just below. The original award was excluded from that total. A narrow early distinction was followed by a much wider difference in further funding.[^chapter_01-c1-5]

An applicant just above the funding threshold had won; one just below it had lost. Comparing those nearby applicants reduced the differences that would separate the entire pool of winners from the entire pool of rejected scientists. Their talent could still vary. The study's design and assumptions made it possible to investigate the award's effects without assigning the whole later gap to initial superiority.

The competitions had names: Veni for the early-career award and Vidi for the midcareer grant. Among applicants closest to the Veni threshold, about 59 percent of winners later applied for Vidi, compared with 40 percent of nonwinners. The researchers checked whether the application difference simply reflected people leaving academia or the country; it did not. An earlier decision had changed which proposals reached a later panel, before that panel judged anything.[^chapter_01-c1-5]

A scientist can stop applying, a supplier can stop bidding, and a musician can stop trying to reach an audience. The alternatives do not wait patiently for evaluation. An apparently thin field may reflect earlier decisions that drove people away. The missing proposal is harder to inspect than one that received a low score.

There are several possible practical responses, and they need different evidence. Helping promising unsuccessful applicants reapply would address participation. Giving a reviewer a clearer account of how close a prior decision was might affect the interpretation of a failure. Funding everyone would involve a much larger commitment with different costs. The study makes a mechanism visible; choosing a response requires asking which change would improve the institution's work.

Subsequent funding gives a scientist resources for further work. The discoveries made with those resources are another outcome. More money might permit important research, but the size of the grant cannot tell us how much scientific value it produced. An institution can create a gap in resources and a gap in recognition without those gaps carrying identical information about achievement.

The comparison with the online comment is instructive. An artificial vote changes the evaluation of fixed words. A grant can help change the future words, experiments, and projects themselves. Both involve feedback, but the productive consequences need not be the same. Understanding cumulative advantage requires following what the reward does after it is received, rather than treating attention, money, equipment, and achievement as interchangeable quantities.

### What failure selects

A story about early advantage should be able to survive an encounter with a successful loser. In a 2019 study, Yang Wang, Benjamin Jones, and Dashun Wang compared junior scientists whose applications narrowly received or missed US National Institutes of Health funding. Early rejection was associated with greater departure from the NIH system. Among those who remained active, however, near-misses subsequently produced work with greater citation impact than narrow winners.[^chapter_01-c1-6]

Among those who stayed, some did distinguished work after an early rejection. Others left the funding system. Judging the setback only by the survivors would omit those departures; judging it only by departures would omit the later achievements. Both groups were part of the original applicant pool.

For a scientist still submitting proposals, the ability to continue may be part of what distinguishes that person from someone who stops. Later success among survivors does not by itself show that giving everyone the same early setback would improve scientific work. The researchers investigated whether selection alone could explain their findings and argued that it did not fully do so.[^chapter_01-c1-6]

Imagine reading a shelf of papers by researchers who overcame rejection. The papers are real achievements. The biographies might offer useful accounts of persistence. But the shelf contains no place for papers that were never written by people who lacked the resources, inclination, or opportunity to continue. Admiring the work on the shelf and evaluating the institution's treatment of the original applicants are different tasks. One requires attention to achievements; the other also requires attention to absences.

This is the same discipline we needed for Lockdown's missing chart. An outcome can be impressive without revealing every route by which the observed group came to exist. A scientist's later work may genuinely be better. The additional question is what an observer can infer about a process that also affects who remains visible. The existence of a successful survivor cannot, unaided, make the cost of the missing careers disappear.

Nor should we turn the funding result into a convenient reversal of the Matthew effect. Further funding and citation impact measure different outcomes. A person can receive less money and produce influential work. Another can receive more money for research that is valuable in ways the chosen citation measure does not capture. The question of which reward predicts which achievement must remain specific enough for evidence to answer it.

Subsequent researchers tested both patterns across a wider set of funding programs. A 2025 reviewed preprint by Vincent Traag and colleagues examined fourteen programs from six funders in Europe and North America. It found the relationship between early and later funding more robust than the apparent advantage in later citation impact following rejection. The latter varied with setting and method; selection into reapplication offered an important explanation.[^chapter_01-c1-7]

That is useful news for anyone tempted by a universal story. A striking finding about one group can identify a mechanism worth studying without becoming a rule for all careers. Replication can change our confidence about how far it travels. If a comparison is valuable enough to influence policy, it is valuable enough to examine under arrangements that might reveal its limits.

For a funding agency, the practical choice is not whether to believe that winners are talented or that losers are resilient. It is how to allocate resources while learning what those allocations do. Does an early award change later applications? Does it help people do better work? Does a rejection remove promising candidates from future consideration? Different records are needed for the different answers. A single later honor roll cannot contain them all.

An early rejection need not settle a scientist's career. Another funder, laboratory or employer can provide a route back. People respond to setbacks, and institutions can revise earlier judgments. Which opportunities remain available, to whom, and for how long helps determine whether an early disadvantage persists.

### The road with the fuel

Long before online charts, Armen Alchian offered an economical way to separate a successful outcome from a wise decision. His 1950 paper imagined thousands of travelers leaving Chicago along randomly chosen roads. Gasoline stations existed on only one road. Travelers who happened to choose it could continue; the others would run out of fuel. The continuing travelers might look unusually far-sighted to someone who saw only their progress.[^chapter_01-c1-alchian]

Alchian's environment selected surviving behavior without requiring every individual to understand the selection in advance. If gasoline supplies moved to another road, the pattern of continued travel would change even without travelers changing their paths.[^chapter_01-c1-alchian]

The travelers made real progress along the fueled road. Someone who saw only their continued movement might mistake survival for foresight. Knowing how they chose their roads changes that explanation without changing the distance they traveled.

In the grant studies, the scientist who keeps applying remains eligible for later awards. The one who stops no longer appears in the same competition. Their continued presence or absence says little on its own about the earlier experiences that changed their choices and opportunities.

If the surviving travelers also learned along the way, their lucky route could support real improvements in skill. Selection and learning could then operate together. An early advantage can make productive work possible without measuring how much that work caused the eventual reward.

For a Wikipedia editor, recognition can bring another person to a useful contribution. For a business, revenue can finance improvements. These are different mechanisms. Alchian's example exposes an inference from survival; explaining a particular reward system still requires investigating how it selects, rewards, and develops its participants.

Markets can select viable activities under uncertainty. People can imitate, adapt, and learn without possessing a perfect account of the economy. Choosing a business that works is one task; measuring how much each input contributed to its rewards is another. The first can be useful while the second remains unresolved.

The response should be to examine what would discriminate between explanations. In the travelers' example, discovering where the fuel was located changes the interpretation of the journey. In the online experiments, the researcher's record of the assigned advantage changes the interpretation of the later score. In a real procurement process, a useful independent trial might do similar work. Each additional observation earns its value by answering a question the winner's progress could not settle alone.

We can therefore learn from a successful traveler without treating every mile as a certificate of foresight. The same discipline lets us learn from a successful company without turning every sale into a complete causal audit. It asks us to understand the selection process as well as the selected outcome, and to notice which comparisons that process leaves available for the next observer.

### The car the buyer can inspect

Akerlof's used-car model makes another kind of missing evidence easy to picture. A seller learns whether the car is good through ownership. A prospective buyer cannot distinguish its quality as well. The offered price reflects that uncertainty, and a good car's owner may refuse to sell at it. The information problem can remove good cars from the set the buyer gets to choose among. Quality and selection interact.[^chapter_01-c1-8]

The buyer's uncertainty helps determine which cars actually appear in this market. Inspecting only cars offered at the resulting price would not show the full population of cars their owners might have sold under different information arrangements. The missing good car matters even though no transaction involving it appears in the sales record. Akerlof's example illustrates how a market's rules of information can help select its observable contents.

### What was in the bottle

I encountered that difficulty selling olive oil. The business failed in 2026. We had a premium product with tight margins. Donald Trump's tariffs on imports into the United States added to the cost of reaching our customers.[^chapter_01-c1-14] Rising fuel costs reached us at more than one stage: air or sea freight into the country, then the last mile from a third-party fulfillment center to the buyer. A bottle had to pay for the whole journey.

I spent a long time trying to drive costs down. But there was a limit to what business optimization could accomplish. Replacing some of the olive oil with a cheaper seed oil would reduce a cost by changing what was in the bottle. Selling that mixture as pure olive oil would make the customer's comparison false.

Tom Mueller explored that market in *Extra Virginity: The Sublime and Scandalous World of Olive Oil* in 2011. Discussing the book on *Fresh Air* that December, he described what happens when cheap, inferior oil and carefully produced oil share a label: “the honest people are getting terribly undercut.”[^chapter_01-c1-12] The FDA also identifies dilution with cheaper vegetable oil sold as pure olive oil as a form of food fraud.[^chapter_01-c1-13]

A buyer who cannot reliably distinguish the contents sees a price difference that may conceal a product difference. The honest seller then has another expense: persuading someone that the difference is real. I felt that burden in the money we needed to spend on advertising while freight and delivery were consuming what margin remained. A cheaper sale elsewhere could mean greater efficiency, a different product, or a misleading claim. The label alone could not settle which. Testing and credible enforcement matter because a rule on a bottle has to mean something outside the bottle.

### What an inspection can settle

Suppose an inspection now tells the used-car buyer each car's condition accurately. That resolves a question about quality. It still does not tell a dealership how much its advertising added to sales, how much a salesperson's work affected a customer's choice, or how a prior reputation brought that customer through the door.

An inspection can enable a trade by answering a valuable question about the car. Measuring how much each input added to the dealership's income requires further evidence. A certificate of condition and an estimate of contribution are different products, even if both are called information.

eBay's paid-search study provides a real counterpart. There was no need to doubt that an advertisement had appeared or that a customer had clicked. The records could establish the event. The experimental suspension showed how much apparent advertising success could be supplied through another route instead. The question was about an effect, and the added evidence came from changing the conditions under which that effect could be observed.[^chapter_01-c1-9]

It helps to picture the two records side by side. In one, the customer follows the paid link. In the other, the customer can reach the same destination without that advertisement. We are interested in the difference in purchasing, rather than the mere existence of the first route. A detailed account of the first route is useful for running a website. The second arrangement is useful for investigating what the advertisement contributes.

Broader searches required another test. Someone typing “cell phone” need not have eBay in mind. Using Google's geographic bidding system, the researchers stopped nonbrand advertising for sixty days in 68 US market areas, selected using a random draw followed by matching on past sales. Sales could then be compared with places where advertising continued. New and infrequent customers responded more than frequent customers. An advertisement could be useful to a shopper without creating the purchase that its click would later appear to explain.[^chapter_01-c1-9]

This is where a familiar response to disputes about success reaches its limit. Show the work. Publish the records. Demonstrate that the product functions. Those are useful demands, and sometimes they settle the question actually being asked. But they establish contribution only to the extent that the observations distinguish the relevant alternatives. When the dispute concerns how much a factor changed an outcome, evidence that the factor existed can leave a substantial part unresolved.

In MusicLab, everyone could in principle inspect the same recording. The result did not require one world to receive a secretly defective version. The experimental setting separates the song from some of the history through which listeners encounter it. A recording can therefore be known and its possible market outcomes still varied. This is the observed-input question in a form that does not depend on concealing the product.

Making a useful product establishes an achievement. Measuring how much of its maker's income that achievement caused requires a further comparison. Respect for the work can coexist with uncertainty about the size of the reward attributable to it.

### What success makes possible

An early advantage can finance productive capabilities that become valuable in their own right. Experience with customers can improve a product; revenue can pay for research. Later success may then have better immediate reasons than the original allocation did.

The improvement does not identify its own origin. Opportunity and the response to it have acted together. Another recipient might have developed an equally useful capability, a worse one, or something different. Observing the actual improvement cannot choose among those possibilities.

The work performed along the successful path remains real. A useful explanation must account for both its productive effect and the circumstances that made it feasible. Labeling the first advantage luck cannot measure the later work away; documenting that work cannot establish what a different initial allocation would have produced.

Obtaining the missing comparison can be expensive. Switching systems, training staff, or running an experiment consumes resources and may interrupt reliable service. The economic question is which uncertainty matters enough to justify investigating, and whether a smaller test can resolve it.

Bell's patent opening makes the distinction between knowledge and opportunity concrete. The historical study found that access to inventions and access to customers were different constraints. Outsiders could receive permission to use knowledge while remaining unable to turn it into a market record in the obstructed sector.[^chapter_01-c1-10]

Recognition can invite attention to existing work; revenue can finance new capability. Both change the record the next evaluator encounters. Treating every later award as a fresh judgment on unchanged starting abilities misses how earlier awards shaped the conditions of judgment.

The grant studies reveal another response: participation itself can change after an early result. When preparing an application is costly, repeated rejection can make another attempt unattractive. The observed field of candidates is partly an outcome of the reward process, rather than a fixed list on which that process merely acts.

By then a directory of remaining suppliers may conceal as much history as it reveals. It tells the buyer who is available now. It does not show all the firms that could have developed under different earlier allocations. This is one reason concentration deserves careful description. A smaller field could reflect superior performance, productive scale, restricted access, discouraged entry, or several influences together. Counting the survivors cannot settle the account of how they became the survivors.

### Turning the chart upside down

Salganik and Watts devised a particularly revealing challenge to the idea that a displayed record simply reports what an audience wants. In another MusicLab experiment, published in 2008, they allowed a popularity order to develop and then reversed the displayed download counts in two new worlds. A song with many downloads was presented as having few; one with few was presented as having many. After this intervention, new downloads were recorded honestly.[^chapter_01-c1-11]

The swap could be remarkably concrete. Before inversion, She Said by Parker Theory had 128 downloads and Florence by Post Break Tragedy had nine. In the altered displays, their apparent counts were exchanged. No band needed to improve a recording for the screen to give later listeners a different account of its success. The researchers had changed the history people believed they were entering.[^chapter_01-c1-11]

The first MusicLab experiment followed independently developing histories under unchanged arrangements. The later experiment deliberately altered the popularity record to test how people responded. It changed the chart that listeners saw, even though the recordings stayed the same.

Some apparent popularity became self-fulfilling. Yet the inversion did not permanently reverse the market as a whole. The songs with the greatest independent appeal tended to recover their popularity. The manipulation also reduced overall downloads. The result supports influence while showing that influence encounters preferences and can affect how much people take from the market, as well as which song receives their attention.[^chapter_01-c1-11]

That recovery is an important part of the ending. The best songs were not helpless objects waiting for a number to tell them what they were worth. Listeners still heard recordings and responded to them. A useful argument about history needs space for those responses to overcome some disadvantages. Otherwise, it would have no way to explain why the inversion did less than a fully self-fulfilling account might predict.

The lower download total raises a separate question about the whole market. A band could benefit from a favorable display while listeners collectively found less music they wanted to keep. An individual's rank and an audience's total enjoyment need not move together. The experiment measures downloads rather than every aspect of welfare, but it makes clear why explaining who wins is different from evaluating the outcome for everyone affected.

An early donation, a positive vote, and a research grant do more than change a score. They can change who receives attention, who improves, and who tries again. The later ranking records those consequences together with the qualities people originally brought to the contest.

Another thousand choices will not necessarily separate those causes. They could give another candidate a serious trial, or keep sending opportunities down the route already favored. A record of transactions counts what happened. Explaining success also requires an account of the comparisons those transactions made possible.

---

<a id="chapter_02"></a>

# Chapter 2

## The comparison budget

### A movie behind several doors

The film is Good Will Hunting. One image emphasizes Matt Damon and Minnie Driver; another emphasizes Robin Williams. In a 2017 account of artwork personalization, Netflix engineers used these possibilities to illustrate a choice their system had to make. A member drawn to romance might respond to one invitation, a member drawn to comedy to another. The same film could be introduced through different parts of its appeal.[^chapter_02-c2-1]

There is a small economic experiment hidden in that image. Showing an invitation supplies a chance for somebody to respond. Showing a different invitation supplies a chance to learn something else. An artwork that remains in a designer's folder can be beautiful, but the folder does not contain the audience's response to seeing it. An additional play following the established image does not automatically answer the question about the image still in the folder.

Netflix's engineers described a practical response: deliberately vary some selections, record how they were selected, and use responses to improve later choices. They explained that learning could carry an immediate cost when a member received an image other than the one currently predicted to work best. Their account concerned how to combine that experimentation with personalization, rather than simply displaying the same apparent winner indefinitely.[^chapter_02-c2-1]

A company with a large audience can create comparisons inside its own service. The same scale that helps an established choice dominate can spread the cost of testing another. What matters is how the service assigns those opportunities.

The image also keeps us honest about the question. Comparing two invitations to watch Good Will Hunting would not measure the contribution of every actor, the director, the screenwriter, or the company carrying the film. A useful experiment can answer a narrow question very well. Its strength depends partly on resisting the urge to attach every neighboring question to the same result.

The engineers also asked what happens when an image changes between visits. A member might remember a film by its picture and then fail to recognize the new invitation. Or the replacement might finally persuade the member to try it. If several pictures preceded a play, deciding which deserved credit became harder. The team treated the frequency of image changes as part of the learning problem.[^chapter_02-c2-1]

Getting somebody to press play was not enough, either. A striking picture could attract an audience that soon abandoned the film. The engineers described using the quality of engagement when labeling their observations, to avoid rewarding that kind of clickbait. Now the record needed to connect the member, title, image, and subsequent response. A million presentations would be less revealing if the file lost those connections.[^chapter_02-c2-1]

The comparison budget counts accumulated allocation chance outside the current favorite. It connects the distribution of opportunities to the evidence those opportunities can produce. Two services with equally busy transaction logs can leave very different scope for learning about productive contribution.

### What we add up

Start with two alternatives that each have a one-half chance of receiving an allocation. Either can be designated the current favorite when they tie. The chance outside it is one half, which becomes the first addition to the comparison budget. This is a probability before the result is known, rather than a prediction of the realized winner.

At the next allocation, suppose the leading alternative has a three-in-four chance. The chance outside it contributes one quarter. If the other alternative becomes the favorite at a third allocation with the same margin, the addition is again one quarter. Across these three decisions, the additions total one.

That total does not promise exactly one outsider victory. The awards could repeatedly go to the favorite or sometimes go elsewhere. A quarter added before an allocation stays a quarter in the accounting, whichever alternative wins.
