The favorite is redefined at every step. The quantity concerns the concentration of the next opportunity, conditional on the history, rather than loyalty to a permanent incumbent.

The favorite is defined by the chance of receiving the allocation, not by an independent verdict that it is the best product. A buyer can favor a familiar supplier because switching is costly. An experimenter can deliberately give a plausible challenger substantial attention. The reason for the choice and the quality of the option both matter economically, but neither should be smuggled into the word "favorite." The budget first describes how opportunities are distributed.

Calling this a budget does not mean that somebody deposited a fixed fund of experimental tokens at the beginning. The quantity accumulates as opportunities arrive. We can ask how much has accumulated so far, or what the remaining sequence could add under a specified process. The economic issue is whether continuing the market continues to supply meaningful possibilities outside its current favorite.

With three alternatives, an eight-in-ten chance for the favorite leaves a combined two-in-ten chance outside it. Adding another name changes nothing if it receives no chance and the existing probabilities remain the same. A longer directory is not itself a larger comparison budget.

A company can preserve a variety of informative trials while remaining a single company, as Netflix's artwork experiments show. For image selection, the relevant alternatives are the pictures users could see. Counting corporate registrations would count something else.

The comparison budget describes opportunities through which evidence can emerge. It leaves service quality, price, workers' incomes, and consumers' enjoyment to be assessed separately. Its economic value depends on what those opportunities reveal and which decisions the evidence improves.

The allocation needs a consistent unit. For a study of image selection, candidate images could be the alternatives and a presentation the allocated opportunity. Whether somebody subsequently plays the film is an additional outcome. For a procurement study, suppliers and contracts could take those roles instead. We cannot count presentations for one question, switch to completed plays for another, and call the resulting total the same budget. The definition becomes useful by fixing the economic question and the process before counting.

When verified productive inputs enter the allocation rule in my model, and their differences remain within the stated bounds, the comparison budget places an upper limit on the information the allocation history provides about their effects. Scarce outside chances can therefore limit how precisely contribution can be recovered from the record. A large budget can still leave attribution unresolved if the trials never distinguish the relevant inputs.

### The news story that was not shown

Yahoo's front page gives us a documented example of an organization designing a record for later comparison. In research presented in 2010, Lihong Li, Wei Chu, John Langford, and Robert Schapire studied personalized news recommendation. Part of the underlying traffic came from a randomized bucket: candidate articles were selected at random for the highlighted position, with user and article information and the resulting click or nonclick recorded.[^chapter_02-c2-3]

The unshown article creates the difficulty. A person encounters one highlighted story, and the system sees whether that person clicks it. The same encounter does not reveal whether the person would have clicked every other candidate. The researchers' procedure started from that limited observation, rather than treating a visitor's arrival as a completed test of every story in the pool.

Random selection made a different use of the record possible. A proposed recommendation rule could be evaluated against logged encounters in which its choice matched the article actually selected. Under the paper's assumptions, this replay procedure supplied a reliable way to evaluate the rule using existing experimental traffic. The record was useful because of how the articles had been chosen and which responses had been retained.[^chapter_02-c2-3]

It is a beautiful answer to one version of the data problem. The observer does not invent a visitor's reaction to an unseen story. The evaluation uses reactions to stories that were genuinely shown. Its credibility comes from the logging design and the assumptions connecting those encounters to the proposed rule. The large database helps, but the relevant form of variation was built into the process before the later analyst opened it.

Now imagine a different file in which every visitor had been shown the same predicted favorite. The analyst might still learn much about that story and those visitors. What would be absent is the same coverage of choices under the randomized design. Replaying another rule would require further assumptions or further data. A more powerful computer could search the file faster without putting the missing article in front of a visitor who never saw it.

Yahoo recorded what visitors did after an article appeared, including nonclicks, as well as information about each visitor and article. A study of contribution has to use that evidence alongside the selection record. Counting only which story won the position would discard observations the experiment was designed to collect.

A replay based on yesterday's visits cannot, by itself, tell us how a new recommendation rule would change tomorrow's audience. Readers might return more often; publishers might offer different stories. Studying those changes requires a design or model that accounts for those responses, rather than assuming that the same encounters would keep arriving.

Imagine four groups of viewers, each always assigned its own particular image. Across an equally sized population, the images would have equal shares of presentations. Yet within a group, the rule leaves no chance for another image. Aggregate diversity can hide a complete lack of alternatives within each group.

The converse is possible too. One image could dominate overall presentations while a carefully selected trial supplies useful evidence about a question involving another audience. The minority share alone would not tell us whether that evidence is weak or strong. We would need the selection rule, the groups involved, and the responses observed. Equal totals can conceal different processes; unequal totals can coexist with purposeful learning. Neither pattern in a chart substitutes for inspecting how each opportunity arose.

An allocation record can contain more than the winner. Task characteristics, eligibility terms, selection chances, and measured outcomes may supply additional information. Those observations should be included explicitly, even when they answer questions outside the simple allocation model.

### A connection someone gets to make

A recommendation can also change a person's economic network. Karthik Rajkumar and colleagues studied randomized variations in LinkedIn's People You May Know recommendations. Different variants changed the kinds of connections suggested. The research, published in 2022, linked these experiments to subsequent job mobility. The effects of weak connections depended on how tie strength was measured; simply seeking ever weaker relationships was not a universal prescription.[^chapter_02-c2-8]

The connection makes the sequence particularly tangible. A recommended person can become a contact. That contact can help information about an opportunity reach a job seeker. A subsequent job change is then part of a history influenced by an earlier selection. The study's design allowed the researchers to investigate aspects of this process that an ordinary list of members and their later jobs would have left entangled.

Imagine studying a member's career after the connection has been made. The profile could show qualifications, previous employment, and a real new role. The question about the recommendation is whether changing who was suggested affected the path into that role. The person's abilities need not be fictitious for the route to matter. A useful match can involve both a qualified worker and information that reached that worker through a particular contact.

By varying connection recommendations and following later employment outcomes, LinkedIn's researchers could investigate how an introduction changed someone's opportunities. The useful evidence came from deliberately changing which connections people encountered.

What made a tie weak? The researchers used both the number of mutual connections and the intensity of bilateral messaging. These measures describe different relationships: knowing few of the same people and rarely exchanging messages need not coincide. Moderately weak ties did best by the mutual-connections measure; the weakest ties did best by interaction intensity. Even the practical meaning of trying a different connection depended on the measurement.[^chapter_02-c2-8]

The authors also examined the risk that treating one member could affect another member's outcomes. Connected people can affect one another even when their accounts appear separately in the data. Suppliers sharing a distribution channel can likewise share influences that a comparison has to account for.[^chapter_02-c2-8]

A network can improve access to opportunities while complicating attribution of their causes. References, connections, and observed performance need to be distinguished in an account of how the next allocation occurs.

### Two long futures

Suppose the chance outside the favorite stays at one in a thousand at every allocation. Each opportunity adds a thousandth to the budget. A thousand opportunities add one; another thousand add another one. The process is heavily concentrated, but its accumulated outside chance keeps growing.

Concentration and a finite lifetime comparison budget are therefore different properties. A tiny chance that persists differs from a chance that vanishes quickly enough for its total to remain bounded. Nor does either calculation promise that awards arrive on schedule or reveal an informative input contrast.

Now consider a different, explicitly hypothetical allocation schedule. Start with a one-in-four chance outside the favorite. At every subsequent opportunity, halve that chance. The first few opportunities contribute most of what the entire future can add. Continuing forever adds successively smaller pieces, and all those pieces together amount to one half. There can be indefinitely many decisions while the accumulated outside chance has a finite ceiling.

The contrast concerns the whole sequence. A single photograph of either process could show a dominant favorite and a neglected rival. The later history depends on whether meaningful chances continue arriving or become scarce fast enough. To distinguish those possibilities empirically, we need evidence about the rules and their evolution. A concentration chart is a record of realized shares, not a measurement of that future sequence.

A third schedule makes the need for care clearer still. Let outside chances decline, but so slowly that the additions continue accumulating without limit. We do not need the arithmetic of that schedule to see the conceptual point. "Shrinking" is not a synonym for "finite in total." Before a claim about disappearing comparison does serious work, the rate of disappearance must be established in the setting being discussed.

The halving schedule is an accounting illustration, not a fitted model of a named company. An actual supplier's prospects could rise after a useful demonstration or fall after an incompatible product change. Its future chance could depend on who won earlier contracts. The budget follows the relevant conditional chances through those changes. Replacing that evolving process with a single fixed market-share number would remove precisely the history the object is meant to describe.

The schedules expose a distinction ordinary language can blur. A very small chance may feel equivalent to none when deciding whether to make one costly attempt. For what an indefinitely long record can reveal, persistence matters: a long sequence can accumulate what each opportunity adds only in a tiny amount.

A business, worker, or policymaker may need an answer long before an indefinitely accumulating process becomes helpful. An infinite lifetime budget could coexist with painfully little relevant opportunity over five years. A trial available after the purchasing decision cannot inform that purchase.

Nor can the opposite inference be made automatically. A finite amount of opportunity need not leave every practical question unresolved. A buyer might already have enough evidence to choose a service sensibly. The stronger issue is a promise of reliable, increasingly precise attribution throughout the admissible explanations of the model. That is a more demanding promise than choosing an option that works well with the information currently available.

Netflix might need to choose an image for a member; a public buyer might need to decide whether another supplier merits a pilot. Each can act with some uncertainty remaining. The useful comparison is one that could improve the decision enough to justify its cost.

### How a lead can close a contest

There is a long mathematical history behind the difference between feedback that preserves comparison and feedback that eventually overwhelms it. One classic model begins with an urn holding colored balls. Draw a ball, return it, and add another of the same color. A color selected early becomes more likely to be selected again. Robin Pemantle's survey describes this family of reinforced processes and the many different behaviors produced by their rules.[^chapter_02-c2-4]

The urn isolates a feedback rule: an earlier draw changes the chances of later draws. Changing that rule can change how the sequence develops, even when every version can be described casually as success breeding success.

In the familiar urn, adding a ball of the drawn color can change long-run proportions while other colors continue to appear. Unequal histories can still keep producing comparisons. The rule governing each addition determines whether the outside chances have a finite lifetime total.

A stronger version gives accumulated success a much more powerful effect on the next selection. For a simple example, imagine weights that grow with the square of the accumulated score: doubling a score quadruples its weight. Research on appropriate strong-feedback models establishes a striking outcome. After some finite, random point, a single alternative receives every subsequent allocation. The result depends on the specified reinforcement conditions, not merely on a large lead.[^chapter_02-c2-5]

Roberto Oliveira studied the onset of such dominance, building on earlier monopoly results. Thomas Gottfried and Stefan Grosskinsky later distinguished several feedback regimes, including strong and weaker forms of monopoly. Their results explain how different reinforcement rules can produce different forms of dominance.[^chapter_02-c2-5]

In the strong-feedback model developed here, the remaining chances outside the eventual winner have a finite total. The comparison budget therefore stays finite. Under the other conditions of the attribution result, the same process that concentrates awards also limits what their history can reveal about productive contribution. Dominance describes who keeps winning. The information bound concerns what those wins allow an observer to learn.

There is a feature here worth holding onto. An alternative can retain a positive chance at every finite stage, yet receive no further awards along the realized history. A positive chance is not a promise of an eventual event. When those chances get small quickly enough, their continuing existence need not create an endless supply of informative deviations. Nominal eligibility and sustained practical contestability can therefore diverge within a precisely specified model.

Entry, new products, new tasks, or independent tests can reopen comparisons. Whether positive feedback closes them permanently depends on the allocation rule and which changes remain possible. Gottfried and Grosskinsky distinguish reinforcement processes with different long-run behavior; the rule determines which result applies.

The urn and Netflix's artwork system both use selection rules that change with earlier results. The urn makes the feedback easy to inspect. Netflix shows how a business can design those rules to keep learning.

### The calls made to choose a policy

An experiment with rice farmers in India gives the selection question a different purpose. Maximilian Kasy and Anja Sautmann worked with Precision Agriculture for Development on ways to enroll farmers in a phone-based extension service. Six strategies combined morning or evening automated calls with different advance text-message arrangements. The recorded success was completion of five questions during the call. It was not a measure of a farmer's eventual harvest.[^chapter_02-c2-6]

The experiment proceeded in waves. Results from completed calls informed assignment to later strategies. The aim was to help select an approach for use after the experiment. That purpose matters: maximizing the number of completed calls during the trial is a different objective from collecting the evidence needed to make a better choice for the much larger implementation that may follow it.[^chapter_02-c2-6]

The distinction is familiar once we put a calendar beside the experiment. This week's calls can deliver useful service. They can also teach the organization which method to use next month. Giving every remaining call to the current leader may improve the immediate recorded success rate while leaving a close challenger insufficiently tested. A reasonable allocation depends partly on whose outcomes, at which dates, the organization is trying to improve.

Kasy and Sautmann's exploration-sampling method retained substantial testing of plausible alternatives instead of simply concentrating on the strategy most likely to be best. Results from earlier calls helped determine which strategies received later ones.[^chapter_02-c2-6]

The case supplies an important counterweight to the idea that experimentation must mean equal shares forever. Once an approach looks clearly unpromising, continuing to assign it the same share may be a poor use of opportunities. A close challenger may deserve more investigation. Relevant comparison depends on which uncertainty remains, how much resolving it would matter, and the consequences of sending another call along each route.

Adaptive design ties opportunities to the uncertainty being investigated. A limited experiment on a relevant task can distinguish plausible explanations; a nominally balanced allocation on incomparable tasks may add little. The assignment process must be related to what the observer wants to learn.

The measure of success should remain visible as well. Completing five questions is something an organization can record promptly. It is useful for choosing among recruitment methods on that outcome. If the later decision concerns agricultural income or the quality of advice, additional evidence is needed. The outcome recorded for fast adaptation should not acquire authority over every consequence simply because it arrives in time to guide the next wave.

This is especially important when comparisons have different costs. An ineffective image may waste a small amount of a viewer's attention. An unsuitable software deployment can demand weeks of staff time. A policy pilot may affect people whose welfare matters during the experiment as well as afterward. The agricultural study's stated objective helps identify the decision being optimized; other settings may require a different balance.

An institution can therefore preserve comparison while making increasingly selective choices. It need not suspend judgment until every candidate has been exhausted. The discipline is to state why the next allocation is useful, which possible responses would change the decision, and what costs are justified by that learning. Those questions turn a general preference for experimentation into something a buyer or program manager can actually implement.

### What a large budget cannot buy

Equal chances at every allocation would make the budget grow, but still need not identify an input effect. If the measured input is identical across the alternatives, reallocating awards changes the recipient without creating the contrast needed to learn that input's effect.

For a less abstract picture, imagine testing two artwork files that display exactly the same image. More exposures can help estimate how that image performs with particular audiences. They cannot reveal the difference between the two visual treatments because the treatments do not differ. A system can create a great deal of nominal variation while leaving the intended comparison untouched. Counting chances is necessary for one bound; it is insufficient for a guarantee.

A change of name need not create an independent trial either. Products may share an underlying service, a bottleneck, or a faulty outcome measure. The budget does not certify independence or meaningful variation merely because a different alternative receives an award.

Yaneer Bar-Yam's distinction between large quantities of detailed data and information relevant to an intervention is useful here. His framework concerns how to identify what matters for a system-level question, rather than treating detail as automatically sufficient. The comparison budget addresses a narrower allocation setting, but the shared discipline is to ask what the record can distinguish for the question under study.[^chapter_02-c2-7]

Yahoo's article evaluation depended on randomized logging and observed responses. The farmer calls depended on the measured completion outcome and adaptive assignment. In each case, the record's usefulness followed from how it was generated, rather than its size alone.

A buyer might obtain an independent performance test without awarding another full contract. If that test distinguishes effects the award history leaves uncertain, it adds information to the record. The allocation-only limit then describes only part of what the buyer can observe.

Likewise, known selection probabilities can be useful information, not merely quantities an analyst may assume into existence. A designed experiment may log them. An ordinary market may require estimates based on observed rules and behavior. If obtaining those estimates supplies additional information about contribution, that information has to be counted. An empirically defensible budget needs a defensible observation model alongside it.

Defining the object and measuring it are therefore separate achievements. The rice-farmer experiment could implement assignment probabilities deliberately; an analyst of an ordinary market cannot assume comparable access to its mechanism. Sometimes only a range of plausible budgets will be justified. That can still organize an investigation, provided the range and its consequences remain visible. Precision written into a definition should never be mistaken for precision supplied by observations.

The comparison budget cannot be read from a market-share table. A rival's sales tell us what happened, not its chance on the next job. Even a well-measured budget needs evidence about differences in what suppliers do and how well they perform. Frequent trials would tell us little about engineering if they never compared meaningful differences in engineering.

### A large company that keeps testing

Microsoft's Bing supplies a particularly direct challenge to the idea that the number of firms tells us how much learning remains. In a 2013 paper, Ron Kohavi and colleagues described more than two hundred experiments running concurrently inside the service. Some users were held out to assess the experimentation system's overall effect. The organization was deliberately maintaining differences in experience rather than relying solely on the record of the version everyone already used.[^chapter_02-c2-9]

One example involved adding links within advertisements so people could navigate directly to parts of an advertiser's site. The initial version increased revenue but worsened user metrics and page-loading time. Adjusting the presentation, including how many main advertisements appeared, changed that balance. The investigators were tracking more than a single increase in a commercial total. A result that looked attractive on one outcome still needed interpretation alongside others.[^chapter_02-c2-9]

Keeping only the revenue increase would lose part of what Bing's experiment revealed. Keeping only the company's size would lose the comparisons it arranged. The useful evidence combined an intervention, assignment, recorded responses, and a decision about which outcomes mattered.

The extra links offered a plausible convenience. Testing revealed consequences the designers had not settled in advance. Their skill helped create the proposal; comparison helped decide how to improve it.

A business can test many changes to its own product while leaving independent suppliers little opportunity to demonstrate theirs. Bing's internal trials can improve Bing without answering how another provider would perform with a route to users.

Sometimes the missing comparison calls for another supplier. Sometimes a company can learn more cheaply by changing its own trial or using records it already keeps. The question is which route will reveal something useful about the decision at hand, and what it will cost.

### The price of keeping a door open

Keeping a comparison available has a price. Testing can require moving data, training staff, checking outcomes, and accepting disruption before its result is known. The hoped-for information must justify those costs. Some alternatives will sensibly remain unevaluated because testing them would cost more than the decision could gain.

Netflix's engineers described their artwork setting as favorable partly because exploration costs could be spread across a large membership. The cost still depends on what a person experiences during the test. A setting that makes outside scrutiny difficult can sometimes make internal experimentation economical.[^chapter_02-c2-1]

The practical question is which uncertainty could change a consequential decision. A small reliability difference may justify investigation when it affects years of expensive operation. A difference incapable of outweighing known switching costs may not. There is no economic instruction to maximize the comparison budget everywhere.

Existing records, a shared test environment, or a limited task can sometimes answer a question more cheaply than a full deployment. Whether they do so depends on their design and on the inference required. A lost route has no automatically adequate substitute.

The order matters. Start with the question, identify a comparison capable of answering it, and assess the cost of producing that comparison. Only then decide whether an access arrangement or purchasing rule helps. Beginning with a preferred number of suppliers and assuming that the resulting record will be informative skips the hardest part of the task. The directory can be long while the relevant test remains absent.

Learning can strengthen the case for an incumbent. An artwork experiment can confirm that the established image works better for the relevant audience. An informative test must allow a result to change our view, including by increasing justified confidence in an existing choice.

A trial earns its cost when the expected value of a better decision exceeds the resources it uses. The allocation chances, input differences, and checked outcomes determine what it can teach. A busy market that repeatedly confirms its favorite can leave a consequential alternative unresolved, while a smaller, well-designed test can answer the question that matters.

That makes the production of evidence an economic choice. Another award to the favorite buys work now. A relevant trial of a rival can buy work and information for the next decision. The value of those purchases depends on which possible histories the existing record can distinguish.

---

<a id="chapter_03"></a>

# Chapter 3

## Shadow futures

### One economy, different histories

The same recording can have another history.

In MusicLab, Lockdown finished first in one experimental world and fortieth in another. The separate groups encountered the same recordings but different developing popularity records. Their charts supply observed alternatives to the idea that one realized ranking explains itself.[^chapter_03-c3-2]

A theoretical replay asks us to hold the underlying effects, starting conditions, and response rules fixed while allowing early allocations to differ. Later investment and behavior can then change because they respond to a different history. The economy follows the same rules without producing identical decisions.

Holding every later input fixed would describe another exercise. If success normally finances investment, suppressing that response changes the assumed economy. A replay preserves the rule connecting opportunity and behavior, rather than freezing behavior regardless of what happens.

A different early allocation need not permanently reverse the outcome. An initial lead can fade, be overcome, or compound. A model must allow the original rules and their uncertainties to determine which follows. Otherwise its preferred alternative has been written into the conclusion.

A shadow future is a feasible allocation history over a stated horizon under the same underlying input effects, starting conditions, and response rules. Different events can produce different subsequent behavior under unchanged rules. The object describes histories the modeled economy can generate, including those it never realizes.

MusicLab's groups contained different listeners rather than identical people sent down cloned lives. Its observable parallel markets therefore illustrate historical variation without constituting literal independent reruns of every feature of an economy.[^chapter_03-c3-2]

MusicLab recorded parallel markets. A theoretical replay describes histories implied by specified rules. The first supplies experimental observations; the second helps define what we seek to explain. Estimating the probability of an imagined history still requires evidence about the process producing it.

An ordinary archive contains the investments and outcomes that occurred. It lacks development that would have been financed by revenue never received. That omission becomes important when we ask the realized record to explain the effect of productive inputs on success.

### What changes, and what stays

Changing an input before an allocation is an intervention. Changing the rule that allocates opportunities is another intervention. Both are legitimate economic questions, but neither is the same exercise as replaying the original process under unchanged conditions.

For the engineering question, we might specify an additional month of work on reliability, a particular starting date, and the contracts available over the next two years. We would then compare expected rewards with and without that extra work, allowing later investment and exposure to respond as the model says they would. The result is a contribution measure for that intervention and horizon. It is not simply the difference between whichever two histories happen to interest us.

Consider the customer-support study introduced in the prologue. Brynjolfsson, Li, and Raymond examined the introduction of an AI assistant and measured changes in agents' work, including issues resolved per hour. Their published study found average gains, with larger gains among less experienced and lower-skilled agents. The comparison concerned access to a particular assistance system in a particular workplace. It did not measure each worker's share of the value created by the entire organization.[^chapter_03-c3-3]

A contribution question must specify its target. Does more engineering change reliability, whether customers observe reliability, or the chance of receiving revenue? An intervention on work and one on information about work can have different consequences.

Opportunity can finance investment, and investment can improve performance. Explaining the resulting reward requires tracing that chain. Recognizing the role of an early allocation does not make later productive work disappear.

MusicLab's separate markets help us see variation in success under a common experimental arrangement. They do not tell us the effect of rewriting Lockdown's chorus. To estimate that effect, we would need a design that compared the relevant recordings under suitable conditions. The song's different rankings and the effect of changing the song are related questions, but the first does not supply the answer to the second.

This is why contribution needs an explicit comparison. An additional engineer, a better recording, and a new distribution route are different changes. Their effects may unfold over different periods. A large reward could be sensitive to one and barely sensitive to another. Saying that success reflects contribution leaves these choices unresolved. Naming the change makes the claim something we can investigate.

This prevents explanations from becoming stories chosen after the outcome. Improving an alternative's initial quality, changing its starting position, and changing its early allocations are different operations. Each needs its own place in the analysis.

### The fork in the laboratory

There is a striking predecessor involving an animal with no procurement department. Line Chloé Le Goff and Philippe Soulier studied reinforced choice using Argentine ants. In their laboratory setup, an ant entered a fork, chose a branch, and was removed. Another ant followed. The maze began without a pheromone trail. A trial ended after one hundred choices; the researchers recorded fifty trials.[^chapter_03-c3-4]

Earlier passages could influence later choices through deposited pheromone. The authors considered a model in which the response to the accumulated trail depended on unknown settings. They studied estimation from repeated trials and the limits of estimation from a single sequence. Their paper connected reinforcement with difficulties recovering parameters, even from an indefinitely extended path.[^chapter_03-c3-4]

The ants expose a statistical temptation common to reinforced processes: a longer record of the established route seems to promise an eventual explanation of why it prevailed. Yet continuing that route differs from observing the process begin again.

The fork was carved into white PVC and partly covered by Plexiglas. No food was present during the experiment. Only one ant occupied the apparatus at a time, and it could not return after choosing a branch. The researchers designed these restrictions to make their behavioral assumptions more credible: an arriving ant encountered the trail left by predecessors, rather than other ants at the junction. The apparatus helped define what a choice could tell them.[^chapter_03-c3-4]

The resulting paths did not all look settled. In fewer than ten of the fifty trials, the researchers described a branch as appearing to have been selected. In the others, selection was not obvious after one hundred passages. The same protocol had produced visibly different histories. Those finite records gave the researchers something that continuing traffic down one established branch could not: repeated observations of how a path developed from its beginning.[^chapter_03-c3-4]

Le Goff and Soulier had already shown that a long history of reinforced choices need not reveal the forces behind those choices. In a market, that raises a further question: can the opportunities left open to rivals tell us how much we can learn about why the winner won? The comparison budget gives those opportunities a precise meaning and connects them to the problem of measuring contribution.

The statistical question is how much opportunity the process retains to distinguish input effects. A shorter record can contain substantial relevant variation while a much longer one follows an established route. The design, assignment chances, and measured outcomes determine the difference.

### A million entries are not a million beginnings

Imagine an ordinary coin with an unknown, fixed tendency to land heads. One explanation says heads occurs half the time. Another says it occurs three quarters of the time. Any particular finite sequence of heads and tails is possible under both explanations. Shared possibilities do not make the explanations equally persuasive.

A long sequence containing about three heads for every tail will increasingly favor the second explanation. Under the usual assumption that the tosses are independent with an unchanged bias, the proportion of heads supplies information that keeps accumulating. We do not need to watch every imaginable coin sequence. The one we observe can become enough to distinguish the two fixed biases reliably as the number of tosses grows.

A coin has unobserved possible histories too. One observed sequence can nevertheless distinguish its bias. A lasting attribution limit arises only when the actual process fails to separate the relevant explanations sufficiently as the record grows.

Yahoo's randomized article displays and recorded clicks were designed to support a particular evaluation. The logging mechanism mattered because the researchers did not observe how the same encounter would have responded to every other article. Their analysis used the opportunities the design actually generated, with assumptions about those encounters and the evaluation procedure.[^chapter_03-c3-5]

The complete experiment must be specified. Awards alone supply one information set; measured reliability and independent tests can supply another. A laboratory result directly informative about the input effect cannot be excluded when evaluating what the record reveals.

In the model, the information limit concerns a specified family of candidate explanations for how productive inputs affect allocation. A candidate explanation is not an arbitrary story about the company. It supplies probabilities for the recorded outcomes at each stage. Two such explanations can disagree about engineering's importance while using the same purchasing procedure and the same rules for how suppliers respond to earlier contracts.

The comparison budget then asks how much conditional allocation chance accumulates outside the current favorite. The favorite can change as the purchasing history moves forward. As Chapter 2 showed, a small chance can persist long enough to accumulate without limit. The strongest result concerns the more demanding case in which the lifetime total is finite. It also requires restrictions on how different explanations separate as those chances diminish. Those restrictions specify how far the next-award probabilities can differ between candidate explanations.

To apply the result, an investigator needs to establish the feedback rules and the chances they leave for alternatives. Ant traffic, song charts, and market shares describe outcomes. Establishing a lasting learning limit also requires evidence about how the process generates them.

### When waiting cannot finish the job

First, the candidate explanations must describe a common predictable experiment. Input and state rules are fixed functions of the information available before each award. If success finances investment, each explanation retains that response rule rather than changing behavior to fit the observed record.

Second, the candidate explanations retain the same possible next recipients after every history. If an award is possible under one explanation and impossible under another, that award could decisively distinguish them. The shared-possibility condition excludes this shortcut.

Think again of the coin. Both fixed biases permit heads and tails at every toss, yet the biases can be distinguished in the long run. Shared possible outcomes therefore do not suffice. Third, the model requires the separation between candidate next-award distributions to be controlled by the remaining chance outside the favorite, in each direction of the comparison. As opportunities diminish, the experiment's ability to separate those explanations diminishes with them.

For the bounded input differences in this allocation model, the required control can be established. A separate engineering measurement could nevertheless reveal an input's effect directly. Such evidence must be counted; the allocation record is only one potential source of information.

Fourth, under each candidate explanation the cumulative lifetime chance outside the favorite must remain finite, apart from events assigned probability zero. A dominant observed share or several years of declining rival chances cannot establish that property of the whole process.

The strong-reinforcement models discussed in Chapter 2 supply one sufficient setting under specified assumptions. Ordinary positive feedback does not. A persistent route to trials can preserve accumulating comparison; chances shrinking fast enough can have a bounded lifetime total. Similar observed shares can arise from either arrangement.

Under all these conditions, the complete history cannot support a single contribution-estimation method that becomes reliably accurate under every candidate explanation. For a contribution target that differs across the explanations, no universal procedure eventually recovers the right value everywhere merely by continuing to watch this one history. This includes procedures more sophisticated than simply equating sales with merit. Better calculation cannot create the missing separation.[^chapter_03-c3-6]

The technical step concerns collections of histories. Under these conditions, a collection assigned probability zero by one explanation has probability zero under the other. Their probabilities need not be equal otherwise. A universally reliable method would have to separate histories enough to return different contribution values under different explanations. The model establishes that continuing this experiment cannot deliver that separation. A method could work under one candidate, but cannot promise accuracy under them all.

Under the theorem's conditions, continuing the specified history cannot deliver an attribution procedure whose error vanishes under all candidate explanations. Operational experience can remain useful for other purposes. The limitation concerns the information produced, rather than an analyst's patience.

The result also reaches an apparently modest alternative: avoid estimating a precise number and simply decide which of two different contribution values is correct. Under the same conditions, there is no sequence of such tests for which both kinds of mistake vanish. One cannot escape the attribution problem merely by replacing a detailed merit calculation with a binary verdict.

The obstacle is in the opportunities the process supplies. Under these conditions, even an indefinitely long reward record leaves an attribution problem that better calculation cannot solve. A different experiment could change the answer: independent measurements, informative input variation, or a workable route to rival trials. Waiting for more of the same record cannot substitute for those changes.

### What the record still reveals

The attribution limit leaves many facts intact. Recorded outages, completion times, and accepted work can establish performance. Contracts can establish payment obligations. The result concerns the recovery of a defined contribution effect, rather than a general absence of knowledge.

Even the allocation record can support one explanation more strongly than another. Awards that repeatedly coincide with an observed productive advantage may increase confidence in its effect. The theorem limits the ability to drive attribution error to zero across the specified alternatives; it does not require all explanations to receive equal support.

MusicLab showed substantial variation across worlds without finding that musical appeal was irrelevant. Social influence changed the relationship between appeal and success. Recognizing alternative histories does not require treating every song as interchangeable.[^chapter_03-c3-2]

Execution, predictability, and attribution deserve separate judgments. Demonstrating that work was completed, correctly predicting who receives the next reward, and estimating how much an input changes that reward are different achievements. Strong evidence for the first two can coexist with uncertainty about the third.

Prior information can narrow the range of plausible explanations. A separate engineering test may rule out others. A buyer may also have enough evidence to choose a supplier while remaining uncertain about why the business succeeded. The information needed for that decision can be less demanding than a complete account of contribution.

That uncertainty matters through the decisions it could change. An organization may have enough evidence to renew a reliable service despite unresolved attribution, while an investor assessing a different technology needs the missing input effect. Complete explanation is not a prerequisite for every practical choice.

### Two water supplies, one city

In London during the cholera epidemic of 1854, John Snow investigated a comparison created by two water suppliers. The Lambeth Company had moved its intake upstream in 1852, away from London's sewage. Southwark and Vauxhall drew from a more contaminated stretch. Their customers were intermingled: different houses along the same streets could receive water from different sources.[^chapter_03-c3-12]

Snow sought the water supply of houses where fatal attacks had occurred. This was harder than consulting a tidy customer list. Residents did not always know their supplier. He described using receipts and, when necessary, a chemical test that distinguished the waters. The eventual comparison supported his account of water's role in cholera. The practical work included finding out who had received what.

The inferential opportunity came from a change in supply and the intermingling of customers. Snow did not observe the same household through two literal epidemics, one under each water source. He investigated an existing contrast and argued that the groups' surrounding conditions made it informative. That argument about comparability carried the causal claim; the bare existence of two companies would not have done so.

Before concluding that a record cannot identify contribution, investigators should examine variation supplied by other customers, policy changes, and additional measurements. A result about a sparse observation scheme cannot justify ignoring a richer one that exists.

Snow's example gives verification a precise role. Establishing which water a household received defined the comparison; it did not eliminate the need to assess whether the groups were comparable. Measuring an input and identifying its effect similarly require distinct, complementary work.

An economy need not reproduce its entire past to learn a causal effect. It needs evidence that distinguishes the particular alternatives relevant to that effect. Sometimes naturally occurring variation provides it; sometimes a designed trial helps. The finite-budget argument identifies a setting where continuing the specified record cannot finish that job. It directs us toward better evidence, rather than away from causal investigation.

### Agreeing about the next allocation

Two explanations can converge on the same prediction for the next allocation while assigning different roles to productive inputs and accumulated advantage. Agreement about what comes next need not settle why the process took its present form.

David Blackwell and Lester Dubins established a classical result in 1962 about conditional forecasts coming together as information accumulates. When the observers' probability laws satisfy their absolute-continuity condition, their conditional distributions for the future merge.[^chapter_03-c3-7]

In my finite-budget setting, the fixed candidate history laws have the relationship that Blackwell and Dubins require. Their classical result then applies: forecasts of the future allocation sequence merge, while the differing contribution values remain impossible to recover reliably under every candidate explanation.

Forecasting follows the process already in motion. Contribution asks what an intervention would change within a defensible account of that process. Each can be useful without answering the other's question.

Predictive agreement alone does not establish an attribution problem. Return to the coin with a fixed but unknown bias. Imagine two observers who begin with different reasonable probability distributions over possible biases. Watching the same independent tosses can bring their forecasts together while the observed frequency also reveals the bias. Their growing predictive agreement is perfectly compatible with successful parameter learning.

In the coin example, the fixed half-heads and three-quarters-heads explanations can be distinguished by a sufficiently long record. The observers' initial mixtures over possible biases and the laws generated by those two fixed biases are different objects.

Imagine a forecasting competition that rewards only predictions of the next supplier. It might identify who best anticipates the purchasing process. It would not automatically identify who best understands the effect of an extra engineering month. Those are separate tests. A forecast score becomes evidence about contribution only when the design establishes the relevant connection, rather than assuming that good prediction must explain every cause of the outcome.

### A map with uncertain settings

The distinction between prediction and parameter recovery has another established home: spatial statistics. Hao Zhang's 2004 paper studied models in which observations become more densely packed within a fixed region. Under the conditions he analyzed, not all the relevant covariance parameters could be consistently recovered, even with increasing observation density. Nevertheless, certain predictive conclusions could converge and a particular combined quantity could be estimated.[^chapter_03-c3-8]

Imagine measuring soil across a field and using the readings to predict conditions between sampling points. Two models might increasingly agree about those predictions while leaving some of their settings uncertain. The forecasts could become useful before every detail of the underlying variation was known.

The sampling arrangement matters. Zhang distinguished filling gaps within one fixed region from collecting data over an expanding region. In reinforced allocation, continuing one established history likewise differs from observing additional informative beginnings. More data must be described by how they are generated.

Zhang showed how prediction could improve without identifying every underlying model setting. The allocation question has a related form: whether forecasts of the next reward can become accurate while the effect of changing an input remains unresolved.

A prediction of tomorrow's workload can remain valuable while leaving the effects of more engineering, another supplier, or a different access rule unresolved. The conclusion is about the scope of an inference, rather than the usefulness of prediction itself.

Consider an AI buyer comparing forecasts of its current system's responses with estimates of the benefit of switching systems. Its logs may be excellent for the first problem. They may contain few cases in which another system was tried under comparable conditions. This is a hypothetical purchasing problem, not a conclusion about any named model. Its point is that the relevant comparison follows the decision being considered.

### What a simulation can supply

Repeated runs of a simulation can produce different histories from specified starting resources, allocation rules, input effects, and responses to success. Their distribution reveals what those assumptions permit.

A simulation can expose contradictions, show that an imagined outcome is rare under the chosen rules, or compare the implications of persistent and diminishing exploration. Its conclusions remain conditional on the rules and effects entered into it.

The MusicLab researchers could compare actual recorded outcomes across their experimental worlds. A simulated procurement market does not acquire the same evidential status merely because it contains eight worlds, eight hundred, or eight million. Increasing the number of runs can reduce uncertainty about what the simulator implies. It does not independently validate the simulator's account of how engineering affects contract awards.

Suppose two candidate input effects remain compatible with the evidence. Run the simulation under one and it supplies one distribution of shadow futures. Run it under the other and it supplies another. A precise average inside either simulation leaves the original uncertainty intact: which candidate governs the real procurement process?

Disagreement between defensible models can identify a useful experiment. Similar recommended actions may instead show that a decision is robust to unresolved attribution. Sharply different recommendations make that uncertainty material; displaying only a preferred simulation conceals it.

Yahoo's researchers specified a logging process and assumptions that allowed an existing record to evaluate another article-selection policy. Their replay method used logged encounters to estimate performance. It could answer that question without observing a complete alternative economic history.[^chapter_03-c3-5]

Simulation should distinguish historical variation, input interventions, and institutional changes. All three can be investigated, but their interpretations differ. Shadow futures first specify the economy being replayed.

### A comparison with a purpose

An unobserved alternative could have been superior, ordinary, or unsuccessful. A missing comparison leaves uncertainty among those possibilities. It supplies no presumption that the unrealized outcome would have been better.

When eBay shut off brand-search advertisements, traffic largely shifted to unpaid search results in that setting. Paid clicks had overstated the number of additional customers the advertisements brought. Results for some other searches and users differed, giving the company reasons to change where it spent its advertising money.[^chapter_03-c3-9]

An informative experiment must be capable of disappointing the person who requested it. Results can strengthen an established arrangement or reveal costs that demonstrations concealed. A test designed to vindicate a predetermined winner cannot perform that role.

Estimating the effect of an input also differs from deciding who deserves the resulting gain. Contracts, risk, incentives, and moral claims enter that further argument. Statistical attribution cannot settle them through the word contribution.

A valid contractual claim to payment can survive uncertainty about all the causes of commercial success. A promise and a causal explanation answer different questions.

For competition policy, the relevant question is whether preserving a comparison would improve a consequential decision enough to justify its costs. Users may bear disruption, and some experiments may be uninformative. A missing comparison creates a problem to investigate, rather than a reason to subsidize every alternative.

### Beginning again

One way to learn more is to observe independent repetitions of an informative experiment from a common beginning. Under appropriate identification and regularity conditions, repeated finite experiments can support consistent estimation even when extending one reinforced path cannot.[^chapter_03-c3-10]

Separate organizations do not automatically supply independent repetitions. Shared staff, reputation, shocks, or development can connect their outcomes. Independence and a common informative beginning require a design, rather than a change in letterhead.

MusicLab separated its social markets so that popularity information developed within each world. The design created a way to observe differences a single market would not reveal. Applying an analogous design to suppliers would require identifying which influences actually cross between the proposed units. The useful lesson is about constructing a comparison, rather than giving every administrative subdivision the status of another world.

Repetition must still provide the relevant variation. If measured input profiles are identical across alternatives and no other observation separates their effects, beginning again cannot create the missing contrast. A large budget supplies opportunity; experimental design connects it to the target.

Nor does a repeated experiment have to answer every question. A trial could identify short-run reliability while leaving the consequences of years of accumulated experience unresolved. A buyer might deliberately choose that limited question because it is useful and affordable. The appropriate response is to state the horizon and scope, then use the evidence for what it supports. Modest, interpretable knowledge can improve a decision substantially.

Le Goff and Soulier compared what could be learned from one reinforced sequence with what could be learned from repeated trials. Changing the observation scheme can make a previously unresolved question answerable. In an economic setting, a new test or another measured outcome may supply evidence that ordinary transactions leave out.

### The histories that get a chance

A productive economy can generate different histories, with real work and achievement along each. Shadow futures give those histories a precise place in an explanation of the one observed. Policy then poses the further question of which changes would improve the attainable outcome.

Existing evidence, additional measurements, new experiments, and redesigned allocation rules can support different decisions. The comparison budget describes a constraint within a specified process; shadow futures define histories left outside its realized record.

Bell's 1956 patent decree changed who could use existing inventions. The historical record lets us follow what happened after that opening, including which other barriers entrants still faced.[^chapter_03-c3-11]

The economic task is to identify which comparisons can teach us something valuable, how to preserve them, and when their benefits justify the cost. That task begins before the next winner is known.

---

<a id="chapter_04"></a>

# Chapter 4

## The histories that became infrastructure

### How far can a judgment travel?

A market can grow while the number of independent places to try again shrinks.

Imagine a carpenter who disappoints a buyer in one town. Another town might offer a different buyer, unfamiliar with the dispute and willing to commission a small job. Now imagine that both towns purchase through one platform, which carries the carpenter's ranking from one to the other. The second town is still there. The second judgment may have disappeared. The carpenter's ability to start again depends on whether another buyer can make a fresh assessment.

Adam Smith began his account of the division of labor with a pin workshop. Specialization raised what its workers could produce together. Its reach still depended on finding buyers. In his chapter on the extent of the market, Smith contrasted the narrow opportunities of scattered settlements with the commerce made possible by water transport. A larger reachable market could support work that a small village could not.[^chapter_04-c4-smith]

Distance therefore did two things. It limited the customers an artisan could serve, and it could separate the people deciding whether to give that artisan a chance. Removing distance could expand both income and competition. Whether it also joined previously separate judgments is another question. A new road connecting buyers is different from one authority deciding which sellers every connected buyer may see.

Guilds and settlement rules also obstructed movement in Smith's account. A newcomer could encounter a closed corporation in the next town. Geographical separation offered a fresh assessment only where another buyer had the freedom to make one.[^chapter_04-c4-smith]

Nor did formidable scale begin with software. By 1920, the Supreme Court's United States Steel opinion was recounting a huge industrial combination and disagreement over whether integration reflected the physical requirements of steel production or suppressed competition. Pig iron and finished steel were physical goods; that had not kept their producers small. Furnaces, transport, capital, and organization could already assemble power over a wide territory.[^chapter_04-c4-steel]

The question is how far a common selection system can reach, and what remains outside it. One intermediary serving ten towns may offer customers more choice than ten isolated villages ever had. It may also carry one exclusion across all ten. Increased scale changes the institutional problem when the route to a fresh evaluation disappears.

In 1972, the physicist Philip Anderson called an essay “More Is Different.” Anderson worked at Bell Laboratories, and his subject was how new levels of physical scale and complexity could require new explanations. His title raises a useful economic question: when does an increase in reach change the kind of independence an economy can sustain?[^chapter_04-c4-anderson]

A bigger furnace changes the cost of producing steel. A railway changes where steel can be sold. A shared admissions rule changes which producer can reach those buyers at all. Digital distribution lets an evaluation travel with a seller and be reproduced across transactions at very little cost. The seller can move while the ranking follows. Power supplies and computing clusters remain physically constrained, but one judgment can govern opportunities throughout the network they serve.

Imagine a rival network that needs users before it becomes useful. Those users already communicate through the established network. If the two cannot connect, the entrant may have to assemble a very large customer base before it can offer its first customers a worthwhile service. A network that would work well once established need not have a feasible route from a small beginning. Under those arrangements, a possible competitor can remain economically unreachable. Interconnection could change the arrangements and make a beginning possible.

Increasing returns can give early adoption a lasting influence over which technology develops, as W. Brian Arthur showed.[^chapter_04-c4-6] The question then extends beyond how much cheaper a large producer becomes. How long can its advantage keep compounding before an alternative receives a meaningful test? Where a network's reach also governs access, a successful position can last because the route to challenging it has become harder to build.

That changes what a continuing return tells us. An owner may keep improving the service and also benefit from the absence of a feasible starting point for rivals. No executive needs to cancel a competing project for that structural barrier to work. Capital earns another return, the return finances further expansion, and the alternatives that might have tested the arrangement never acquire a record. The history becomes richer on one side while remaining unwritten on the other.

The next buyer may have every reason to choose the established service. Testing a rival would cost time and might produce a worse result. Other buyers could benefit from what the trial revealed, without paying for it. When a common route closes, that potential learning can disappear across the whole network. The immediate transaction can remain attractive while the opportunity others needed is lost.

Bell's telephone network made permission tangible: a customer could buy an independent attachment, yet risk losing service by using it.

### An invention leaves the laboratory

The transistor existed before the government opened Bell's patents.

In December 1947, John Bardeen and Walter Brattain made a semiconductor device amplify a signal at Bell Laboratories. Their point-contact transistor belonged to a research program drawing on physics, materials, instruments, and the work of several people. In his Nobel lecture, Bardeen emphasized the earlier theoretical work and improvements in materials on which the discovery depended. The invention was an achievement, and it had a history.[^chapter_04-c4-1]

On January 24, 1956, a different kind of event changed what others could do with Bell's inventions. An antitrust settlement required extensive licensing of Bell patents, with thousands of existing patents made available without royalties. The settlement also imposed obligations concerning technical information. It left the Bell System integrated. This was an opening of technology inside a continuing institutional structure, years before the later breakup.[^chapter_04-c4-2]

Between those events lie two questions that are easy to collapse. How did a remarkable invention come into being? What could happen when other people were able to build on it? Bell's accomplishment supplied the starting material for the second question. Recognizing the importance of that accomplishment does not tell us which arrangements would allow the material to produce the most useful subsequent work.

The semiconductor story makes that distinction tangible. A transistor on a laboratory bench is not yet a reliable supply of components, an affordable radio, or a workable design in someone else's product. Moving between those stages requires further decisions and further learning. Whoever gets to undertake that work can discover things the original inventors did not yet know. Whoever cannot undertake it leaves fewer results for later observers to examine.

Accumulated work leaves equipment, procedures, and skills that make some later projects easier. It can also leave permissions and restrictions that determine who may try them. Engineers using an established body of knowledge may work better because somebody has already paid to develop it.

Bell's records let us follow both routes. A technology could leave the laboratory through a license and a training program. A finished attachment could reach a shop yet remain barred from a customer's telephone. The invention, the right to build on it, and the right to use the result were separate economic opportunities.

Follow those opportunities rather than asking whether Bell was simply good or bad. One route led from a major laboratory to inventions others could use. Another led from an independent device to a customer whose telephone service could be threatened if the device was attached. The differences between those routes help explain why releasing knowledge and opening a market can have different consequences.

### The price of a license

Bell had shared transistor technology before the 1956 decree. In April 1952, representatives of forty companies attended a nine-day technology symposium after paying a $25,000 patent-licensing fee. The program included a manufacturing visit. The Computer History Museum's account and collection identify this as part of a broader effort to spread semiconductor techniques. Access did not begin on the day of the settlement.[^chapter_04-c4-3]

That chronology prevents a convenient origin story in which the government released a wholly sealed invention and an industry immediately appeared. A licensing fee, a technical gathering, and compulsory licensing represent different arrangements for passing on knowledge. The relevant question is what each arrangement enabled, for whom, at what cost, and with what further restrictions. “Open” is too broad a description to answer all four.

For an engineer, access can require more than permission to practice a patented invention. There may be a difficult production process to understand, a material whose quality matters, or a component that performs differently outside a demonstration. A legal document can remove a restriction without supplying every skill needed to use the resulting freedom. The distinction is familiar to anyone who has followed a recipe and discovered that instructions and competence are different possessions.

The symposium's combination of licensing and instruction is therefore revealing. Making an invention usable can involve transmitting practical knowledge alongside the rights. The economic object reaching another company is a bundle of opportunities and requirements. A cheaper license helps one part of the problem. It need not remove the other costs of turning an idea into something worth buying.

The government's contemporary account of the 1956 judgment recorded a requirement to furnish technical information at a reasonable charge. It also described annual patent lists and a mechanism for settling royalty disagreements. These were ways of making an access obligation operational. A general statement that firms should be allowed to innovate would have left the mechanics unresolved.[^chapter_04-c4-2]

The incumbent has a legitimate interest here too. Producing instructions, answering technical questions, and maintaining a stable interface require work. An access rule can shift those costs onto an organization that does not receive all the resulting benefits. Ignoring that work would make openness look cheaper than it is. Charging for every missing piece without scrutiny could make nominal access unusable. The design has to address both possibilities.

Bell's case thus begins with an economic sequence rather than a single unlocked door: permission, usable knowledge, practical implementation, and a route to a customer. Each stage can fail even when the previous one succeeds. The next case concerns a device that had already reached the stage of manufacture and sale. Its difficulty lay at the telephone itself.

### A cup on a telephone

Hush-A-Phone was a cup-like attachment that fitted onto a telephone. It was intended to provide privacy for the speaker and reduce surrounding noise. The company had been selling it since 1921. By the time its dispute reached the federal appeals court in 1956, more than 125,000 had gone into use, according to the court's account.[^chapter_04-c4-4]

The telephone companies' tariffs prohibited attachments they had not supplied. They claimed the right to suspend or terminate service for violations. Vendors and users were told that Hush-A-Phone could not be used, and some distributors stopped selling it. The independent maker's ability to build a cup had not secured its customers' ability to use one.

The Federal Communications Commission had upheld the restriction. The appeals court examined the distinction between effects on the conversation and harm to the system. Using the cup for maximum privacy could reduce the clarity and volume heard by the other party. But the court saw no findings supporting the claimed injury to the telephone system generally. It set aside the Commission's order and sent the case back, leaving the antitrust questions open.[^chapter_04-c4-4]

This was a dispute about a mundane object, which is precisely why it is useful. We do not need to settle whether Hush-A-Phone was destined to transform communications. Its claim could be considered at the level of what the device did and whom it affected. A privacy aid might involve a trade-off that the two people in a conversation were capable of choosing for themselves.

A subscriber could cup a hand around the mouthpiece to seek privacy. The court asked why an attachment producing a similar effect should be treated differently. The cup also left the hand free to write. A restriction presented as protection of the telephone system reached down to an ordinary choice about how to hold a private conversation.[^chapter_04-c4-4]

A general network cannot function if every user is free to damage everyone else's service. That concern deserves a technical answer. The question is whether a particular restriction follows from a demonstrated harm, a reasonable precaution, or a blanket preference for equipment supplied by the operator. Those possibilities lead to different rules. They should not be made indistinguishable by calling all of them protection of the system.

When a customer cannot use an independently supplied device, the market produces less experience of that use. Fewer people learn whether it is worth the inconvenience, whether a different design works better, or whether another application is possible. A prohibition can affect the record of performance as well as the maker's immediate sales.

The maker had already sold devices; this was no wholly untested invention. Its complaint went to the FCC in December 1948. Hearings followed in 1950, oral argument in 1951, and a final Commission decision only in December 1955. The appeals court noted the four-year delay after argument. Permission was a question about time as well as principle: an independent producer waited while the established system continued to operate. The court's record supports that chronology, not a calculation of the sales or discoveries the delay prevented.[^chapter_04-c4-4]

A footnote exposed the unequal starting positions. Telephone companies could offer equipment unless the Commission stopped them; an independent maker faced authorization before competing. The court called this unfair and said delay aggravated it, while leaving the legal question unresolved. A permission system can allocate time itself: one side continues accumulating customers while the other waits. That interpretation concerns a regulator's procedure as well as private control. It is distinct from the judgment on the cup.[^chapter_04-c4-4]

### What happened after the decree

Decades later, Martin Watzinger, Thomas Fackler, Markus Nagler, and Monika Schnitzer studied invention after Bell's 1956 settlement. They compared follow-on patenting associated with Bell patents with carefully matched non-Bell patents. Their published finding was a lasting increase in innovation outside telecommunications, while the corresponding increase did not appear inside telecommunications, where customer access remained constrained.[^chapter_04-c4-5]

The design matters because a simple before-and-after count would be difficult to interpret. Semiconductor and other technologies were developing for many reasons. A comparison group provides a way to ask whether the patents affected by the licensing change developed differently from otherwise comparable patents. The credibility of that inference depends on the matching, the timing, and the absence of other differences that would account for the result.

Follow-on patents and their connections to earlier patents capture one dimension of invention. Products that went unpatented, their value to customers, and the achievements of companies that never developed remain outside that measure.

The contrast points to a practical question: can a business actually use the knowledge it is allowed to obtain? A patent license may be worth little if the route to customers remains closed. The Hush-A-Phone dispute makes the telephone network's rules about use tangible.

A buyer may have sound reasons to require reliability, security, and compatibility. A supplier may fail those requirements even with full access to the underlying knowledge. Testing whether an alternative meets a requirement produces evidence; barring the test leaves its performance unknown.

Bell's study measures follow-on patenting after a licensing intervention. To investigate the opportunities behind that result, we would also need to know which teams gained access, what work they attempted, and how their chances of reaching users changed.

The decree was an intervention in the economy's rules. It was not a replay of Bell's earlier history with a different lucky first award. That distinction helps explain what its evidence can do. It can inform our understanding of a change in access. It cannot show us every other route the same unchanged economy might have taken, or give each later inventor an exact share of credit for the resulting industry.

A list of patents is a record of inventions already made. A route to a customer can produce evidence about applications that have not yet been tried. Opening the first does not automatically open the second. This is why an institution can distribute a great deal of existing knowledge while leaving important opportunities for future comparison under the control of the same organization.

### An advantage can become useful

One reason these choices matter is that success can improve the thing that succeeds. W. Brian Arthur's 1989 account of competing technologies made this mechanism explicit. Adoption can bring experience and improvement, attracting further adoption. Under the specified increasing-returns conditions, historical events can help select a durable technological outcome that need not have been the best available development path.[^chapter_04-c4-6]

Suppose, in a thought experiment, a widely used software system has trained operators, tested connectors, and a large collection of fixes. A rival's underlying design might be promising, yet the established system can be the practical choice today. Its accumulated advantages are services users actually value.

Could another history have developed a better set of capabilities? Changing a price at the end may not recreate the development that never happened along the way. A later subsidy can alter incentives; it does not automatically supply years of missing experience.

Bell's technology sharing makes the constructive side visible. Accumulated knowledge can become an input for other people's work. A strong incumbent need not keep every improvement inside its own boundary. An institution may preserve the benefits of a developed technology while allowing others to learn from and extend it. Whether a particular rule achieves that depends on its practical terms.

Historical dependence, increasing returns, and technological dominance do not by themselves establish a finite lifetime comparison budget. To investigate that property, we need the allocation probabilities and an account of how productive inputs affect them.

### The machine and the workforce

The keyboard offers a familiar version of the problem, and a warning about telling it too confidently. Paul David's 1985 paper used QWERTY to explain historical dependence through the interaction of machines, trained typists, and conversion costs. S. J. Liebowitz and Stephen Margolis subsequently disputed his empirical account, including the evidence offered for the advantages of switching. The keyboard is a contested case, not a settled demonstration of an inferior standard trapping the economy.[^chapter_04-c4-7]
