That is evidence about the effect of an initial advantage on later support, rather than evidence that recipients had suddenly become more deserving.[^chapter_06-c6-11]

Now imagine using such a result to design a program supporting new projects. We would want to know which projects the program brings forward, what they produce, and whether the additional donations finance useful work. A finding about subsequent support does not answer all those questions. It gives us a reason to investigate a mechanism that a simple comparison of successful and unsuccessful fundraisers could miss.

One defense of a first payment is that the recipient has already earned it through superior work. Another is that the payment could make a worthwhile undertaking feasible. The second can be persuasive even when the first is uncertain. A public subsidy would still need a worthwhile project and an advantage over other uses of the money.

The value of a proposed experiment is prospective. It should be judged using the information reasonably available when it was funded, including what failure could teach. Later commercial success alone cannot answer whether the original test was worth attempting.

Security can affect participation in experimentation. People unable to meet ordinary expenses after failure may decline a promising attempt. Whether a particular insurance arrangement makes useful experimentation more feasible depends on its financing and behavioral effects.

Claims about risk-bearing deserve specific evidence: what was financed, which losses were possible, which alternatives existed, and what behavior the terms induced. An investment can be genuinely risky at the beginning and later benefit from an arrangement that makes challengers hard to develop. The initial exposure does not establish how competitive every subsequent return remains.

A risk taken once cannot, by itself, justify permanent control over the opportunities future competitors need. A promise of payment can be kept without giving its beneficiary authority to close indispensable routes to independent evaluation. An incentive to attempt the first experiment should leave room for someone else to attempt the next.

Nakamura's account separates the purchases from the learning. The reactor existed before it could grow the material he needed. Its owner financed an uncertain undertaking; modifying it required work that the original purchase did not supply. Nichia's statement emphasizes employer risk, while the lecture describes technical changes. Putting the two documents together makes each claim more concrete without making their eventual settlement a scientific measurement.

There is also a difference between bearing uncertainty and creating it for someone else. In an illustrative procurement system, a supplier might invest in reliability and accept the chance that buyers will prefer a rival. Or it might arrange to make rivals' performance impossible for buyers to assess. Both activities can involve expense. Expense by itself does not establish equal social usefulness.

A charge for destroying valuable comparison would address conduct and its external costs. Its justification would depend on the loss caused and the instrument's effects, rather than the successful supplier's wealth or a rival's complaint alone.

Decent treatment and an entitlement to a contract remain different matters. An opportunity can be humane to preserve yet too unsafe, uninformative, or costly to purchase. Respect for workers includes taking the needs of those relying on the service seriously.

The risk argument becomes more constructive once it stops serving as a badge attached only to winners. We can investigate arrangements that enable useful attempts, allocate losses tolerably, and preserve reasons to work well. We can abandon arrangements that perform badly. The choice is between particular institutions with particular consequences, rather than between reverence for a fortune and indifference to enterprise.

## What a reward should do

What should a reward accomplish? It can encourage useful work, compensate investment, and make a valuable experiment possible. Security against ruin can help someone attempt that work in the first place. A system can pursue those aims while keeping practical routes open for the next capable alternative.

Lockdown can be admired without treating its position in one market as a measure of its makers' worth. A payment promised under an agreement can be respected without insisting that the agreement measured every contribution perfectly. A system that encourages more music need not produce an exact ranking of effort among its winners.

Income support can protect households without generating evidence about their work. An experiment can generate evidence without protecting their incomes. Identifying the intended purpose prevents one benefit from being treated as if it automatically supplies the other.

A popular musician or creator can earn a great deal without controlling anyone else's access to an audience. A platform that decides which creators receive meaningful exposure occupies a different position. The extreme tail of an income distribution does not identify that difference. The relevant question is who can prevent another person's work from receiving a credible hearing.

Changing promises after people have relied on them can damage future cooperation. Prospective rules let investors and workers know which obligations attach to the returns they seek. They also allow the costs and benefits of a change to be assessed before people commit their resources.

Credit needs similar care. Robert Merton's account of the Matthew effect showed how recognition can accumulate around already eminent scientists. Recognizing that mechanism does not require denying the accomplishments of eminent people. It invites us to examine how acknowledgment is assigned and how accumulated standing affects access to further work. His analysis is a predecessor to these questions, not a discovery made here.[^chapter_06-c6-10]

In the LED story, accurate acknowledgment can name particular advances and their authors. It need not wait until economists settle the division of every dollar earned from lighting. When more detailed evidence distinguishes contributions, use it. When it cannot sustain an exact division, praise can remain specific rather than expanding into a claim that all rewards have been morally calibrated.

A similar restraint helps with criticism. A disappointing reward can be evidence of bad rules, weak bargaining power, an unfortunate sequence, poor performance, or several of these together. The possibility of a shadow future does not tell us which explanation fits a particular person. It asks us to investigate before translating low earnings into a verdict on character.

Would loosening the connection between income and merit weaken the motivation to do difficult things? The answer depends on how people respond to a particular change in rewards. A payment can be worth promising because it encourages useful work, even when the eventual income cannot measure exactly how much its recipient contributed.

The driverless-car prize promised payment for completing a demanding course. It did not require a belief that the winner authored the entire technology. The question was whether the incentive was worth its costs. A reward that also grants control over future testing adds another cost: the learning that control can prevent.

Another objection points toward equality. If the rankings could have differed, why not give everyone the same reward? Because the premise does not establish equal contributions, equal costs, equal needs, or identical responses to incentives. Lockdown's divergent rankings unsettle an easy inference from outcome to worth. They do not hand us a complete theory of distributive justice.

Substantial incentives, reliable contracts, and redistribution that gives people security and access to productive assets can be judged by what they make possible. The relevant questions are whether useful work continues, whether people can survive a failed attempt, and whether a capable outsider can acquire the means to begin. None requires an exact moral calibration of the existing income distribution.

Agreement about each person's exact contribution may remain beyond our evidence. Agreement about the next opportunity is still possible. We can choose rules under which accumulated rewards help finance further attempts, while limiting their owners' ability to decide who is allowed to challenge them.

## From the prize to the power cable

An AI supplier can do valuable engineering and commit substantial capital while its eventual rewards also depend on which systems get deployed first, which users encounter them, and which alternatives can afford a credible trial.

Before a computing facility is built, investors need an expectation of returns, workers need compensation, and customers need a service that performs. Society may benefit from capabilities no single payment fully captures. Those needs give reasons to finance construction without making the eventual fortune a complete measure of what caused it.

Keep the light bulb in view. Whatever we decide about the division of credit, semiconductor material still has to be produced and a useful device still has to work. Keep Stanley in view as well. Whatever we decide about the prize, a vehicle still has to complete the course. An economics attentive to possible histories must remain attentive to physical accomplishment.

The economics of an AI fortune begins among chips, cooling systems, and power cables. Long before anyone debates the winnings, someone must build a machine that works.

---

<a id="chapter_07"></a>

# Chapter 7

## The intelligence factory

In the International Energy Agency's 2026 account of artificial intelligence, one of the consequential technologies is a connection to the electricity grid.

The report describes delays that have little to do with a chatbot's ability to write a sentence. Transformers can take years to arrive. In many jurisdictions, a grid connection can involve a wait of five to ten years. A company may have money, a plan, and customers eager to try its service while still lacking a route by which enough electricity can reach the machines.[^chapter_07-c7-1]

This is an unusual setting for a discussion of something described as intelligence. We expect intelligence to concern ideas. Here it also concerns a delivery schedule for electrical equipment. A better algorithm cannot, by itself, carry power down a transmission line that has not been built.

Nor can the phrase data center tell us what a facility will accomplish. A building can house many kinds of computing. The useful economic questions concern the equipment inside it, the work assigned to that equipment, and the arrangements through which its output reaches people. The same physical capacity could support different services and different opportunities to test them.

In its April report, the IEA estimated that all data centers together consumed about 485 terawatt-hours of electricity in 2025. A terawatt-hour is a billion kilowatt-hours, the unit familiar from electricity bills. Its central projection put consumption at roughly 950 terawatt-hours in 2030. That is an outlook, not an observation from the future, and both totals cover more than AI alone. The seemingly weightless answer occupies a material economy.

Someone must obtain the chips, install them, keep them working, and finance the interval before a useful AI product earns anything.

In my own work on cloud infrastructure and software deployments, the practical question is whether something can actually be made to run. Code has to meet the systems on which it depends. A service that works in one setting may need considerable work before somebody else can use it.

The distance between a capability and a working service is a part of the AI story I recognize. Following that distance leads through hardware, power, connections, and the people who operate them.

There are strong reasons for some of that work to occur in large organizations. There are also reasons to ask whether the resulting arrangements let other approaches demonstrate what they can do. Understanding both requires following the power cable into the building.

## The machine needs a building

At Oak Ridge National Laboratory, preparations for the Frontier supercomputer involved a cooling plant, elaborate piping, and foundations capable of carrying cabinets weighing four tons apiece. The laboratory's published construction account records a failed joint in a large pipe during a pressure test. Water poured out; the team identified the failed weld and repaired it. Before the computer could do its work, the building had to survive the plumbing.[^chapter_07-c7-2]

Buying processors had not completed the job. The supporting systems had to pass their own tests. A joint in a pressurized pipe had to hold before the planned operation could proceed. Its usefulness was clear long before anyone could divide the value of the discoveries the machine might support.

Frontier is a scientific supercomputer with that proper name. A frontier AI model is a system near the leading edge of relevant capabilities at a particular time. Such a model is produced and operated on physical systems; it is not the building itself.

Begin with the chips. An accelerator is a processor designed to carry out suitable computations efficiently, often by performing many operations in parallel. Graphics processing units, or GPUs, became central tools for this work. A collection of them does not automatically behave like one enormous, dependable computer. They need data, memory, connections, and software that assigns and coordinates the work.

Imagine a team whose members must repeatedly exchange intermediate results. Adding people may help, but only if they can communicate quickly enough to keep the work moving. A training cluster presents an engineering version of that problem. A useful processor waiting for data or another processor is an expensive object accomplishing less than its specifications suggest.

Cooling creates another coordination problem. The electricity used in computation becomes heat that must leave the equipment. At Oak Ridge, an account of the cooling system describes circulating water warm enough to avoid the refrigeration required by older arrangements. The design uses the fact that water can carry heat away even when it would feel warm to us.[^chapter_07-c7-3]

That small reversal of intuition matters economically. Cooling is a design problem with alternatives, rather than a fixed surcharge attached to every computation. Improvements can change how much useful work a facility gets from a given expenditure. They can require knowledge that is embodied in installation, controls, and operating practice as well as in the chip.

At LUMI, the European supercomputer in Kajaani, Finland, the operator describes another destination for the heat: the city's district-heating network. Its account says excess heat warms hundreds of households annually. The physical by-product of computing becomes an input into another service.[^chapter_07-c7-13]

The important feature is the connection between activities. A facility in one place may be near a usable heat network; another may have no comparable outlet. A household's demand for heating can vary with the season while the computer's workload follows a different schedule. Evaluating the arrangement requires following those actual uses and alternatives.

LUMI's location offers a practical route for moving waste heat to people who need it. Its wider economic benefit depends on the heating it replaces and the equipment and operating costs of the transfer.

That is relevant to a comparison of AI factories even when they offer similar services. Their private accounts can omit different benefits and costs falling outside their walls. A meter reading alone does not identify those effects. Neither does a claim that all computation is virtual. The heat has to go somewhere, and in Kajaani some of it goes into homes.

Counting processors can miss those differences. Counting electricity can miss what the equipment accomplishes. Revenue records what a company receives. Each measure answers a question; none alone explains the whole productive system.

The Oak Ridge construction record makes the missing work visible. Someone had to discover the defect during testing and correct it. That is a contribution we can describe concretely. We do not need an exact division of the supercomputer's lifetime scientific value to acknowledge that preventing a failed pipe from interrupting operation is useful.

Engineering competence can determine whether an AI facility works. Its share in the winning company's eventual fortune is harder to recover. The operating machine demonstrates an accomplishment; explaining the income requires further evidence.

## Keeping the chips working

Meta's technical report on the Llama 3 family provides a view inside large-scale training. For its largest language model, the researchers describe a fifty-four-day period containing 419 unexpected interruptions, in addition to planned ones. Their account also reports more than 90 percent effective training time. A long list of things went wrong, yet the operation continued doing useful work for most of the elapsed time.[^chapter_07-c7-4]

The two facts belong together. Reporting the interruptions alone would make the exercise sound like a collapse. Reporting the useful training time alone would hide the organizational and technical work needed to achieve it. Automation, diagnosis, and recovery turned a collection of fallible components into a functioning undertaking.

A single failed GPU could require the entire training job to restart. A processor that still worked but ran slowly could hold back thousands of others. Meta's report describes tools for finding those stragglers. The coordination problem was physical: many expensive processors depended on the pace and reliability of the components around them. Their number alone did not establish the useful work they could perform.[^chapter_07-c7-4]

The Llama team reported significant manual intervention was needed only three times during that period; automation handled the other issues. An entrant renting well-run computing might obtain the benefit of those procedures without developing them afresh. The service would still need to permit the experiments its product requires.[^chapter_07-c7-4]

Scale can help in several different ways. The work of developing a useful system can be spread across many uses, provided the resulting service remains useful to those users. An operating team can support more than one project. A costly improvement in diagnosis may pay off repeatedly, rather than needing to be rediscovered for every customer.

A second possibility concerns idle capacity. Imagine a shared facility serving customers whose compatible workloads arrive at different times. Combining their demand could keep equipment occupied more consistently than separate facilities would. This is a conditional illustration of an advantage from pooling, not evidence that every large operator uses its machines efficiently or that every workload can be scheduled interchangeably.

Centralizing production need not make downstream services identical. Shared infrastructure can support different research methods and applications. The comparison question follows what can vary above that common layer.

Renting access to well-run computing can reduce the need to duplicate physical infrastructure before attempting a product. The provider's expertise can make more projects feasible. Access terms determine which of those projects can actually proceed.

Requiring each supplier to reproduce the entire cooling plant, network, maintenance system, and staff would buy organizational independence at a considerable resource cost. The extra comparison would have to improve decisions enough to justify that duplication.

An exclusive capacity arrangement can restrict downstream experiments while preserving useful operating expertise. The productive value of a facility and the informational consequences of its allocation rules therefore require separate assessment.

We would want to examine the reason for the reservation. A long commitment might finance construction that would otherwise be impossible. A priority rule might protect work that cannot easily tolerate interruption. Or a restriction might exclude an alternative despite feasible capacity being available. These possibilities require evidence about the particular arrangement; the existence of a large cluster does not choose among them.

Large shared computing is more than a hypothetical possibility. For 2026, the Department of Energy's INCITE program allocated access to seventy-five computational science projects at Argonne and Oak Ridge. Its published account describes scientific peer review alongside assessments of whether proposed work is technically ready for the machines. The organization operating the facility and the teams using it need not be identical.[^chapter_07-c7-14]

The allocations proposed distinct uses of the shared machines, including William Collins's large ensembles of weather extremes using machine learning and research on foundation models for materials. Different teams could apply for resources to pursue different questions; completed results would come later.

INCITE's policy document distinguishes technical readiness from scientific impact. Readiness alone is insufficient for an award. A proposal needing preparation can be considered for preliminary access through another program. A team can need experience with the resource before it can demonstrate readiness for a large allocation.

Shared access replaces one expensive hurdle with another, smaller one: a team can apply for time instead of financing an entire facility. Applications still take work, reviewers can make mistakes, and capacity is scarce. The practical question is which promising projects can now be attempted.

The Llama team learned about its own operation. A failing component, a useful recovery method, or a bottleneck in communication could generate actionable evidence inside the company. Those internal tests improved the operation while leaving other questions about competing products unresolved.

The more demanding question is which question the learning answers. An improved recovery procedure can establish how to keep this cluster operating. It does not, without further work, establish what a differently financed rival would have built or which product a buyer would prefer after a fair trial. A factory can become better understood while its place in the wider economy remains harder to explain.

## Three different jobs

The equipment performs different jobs at different stages. In pre-training, a language model's numerical settings are adjusted using large collections of examples. One common task is predicting text from preceding text. The point is to acquire patterns that support later performance, rather than to write a useful answer for a particular customer at that moment.

Post-training then changes the system further. It can use demonstrations and preferences to improve instruction following, usefulness, or particular capabilities. Meta's Llama report distinguishes these stages explicitly. A system that has learned patterns in text is not automatically the assistant its developer wants to offer.[^chapter_07-c7-5]

Inference is the use of a trained model to produce outputs. A person asks a question; computation produces a response. The work may include additional steps, such as retrieving information or using tools. That use has a cost each time it occurs. Training a model does not eliminate the equipment, electricity, and operations required to serve it.

For an ordinary analogy, designing a machine differs from operating it to make the next item. The analogy has limits because a trained model is a collection of numerical settings that can be copied. But it helps prevent a common accounting confusion: the expense of developing a capability and the expense of using it are different parts of the undertaking.

DeepSeek's report on its V3 model separates pre-training, extending the context the model can handle, and post-training in its cost table. That division tells us that even a reported training figure can combine several activities. It does not represent a timeless price for producing intelligence in every setting.[^chapter_07-c7-6]

Entry can mean training a model, adapting one, or building a service using an existing model. These undertakings require different resources and permissions. A claim about the cost of one cannot stand for the cost of all three.

A supplier that cannot finance a large general training run could still develop a valuable application. A supplier with a powerful model could still build a poor application. The difference might involve reliability, the customer's information, the surrounding software, or the way a worker uses the output. Counting large training runs does not count every opportunity for useful innovation.

Access to inference does not grant control of model development. A product requiring changes to the underlying model may need other permissions, technical capabilities, or a different design. The ability to submit a prompt does not supply every input needed to compete.

The word frontier also needs a task attached to it. A system can lead on one evaluation and be unsuitable for another use. Its position can change as methods and competitors improve. Treating frontier as a permanent title bestowed on a few companies obscures the very discovery process we want to understand.

In its 2026 report, Stanford's AI Index attributes more than 90 percent of the notable models in its 2025 dataset to industry. The dataset is curated for notability; it is not a census of every model or application. It gives evidence of industry's prominent role in this part of development, while leaving open the reasons for that pattern and the consequences of particular access arrangements.[^chapter_07-c7-7]

An assessment of entry must identify the layer being entered and the experiments possible there. A training strategy can be blocked while another useful application remains feasible, or application-level access can coexist with dependence on a single development frontier.

## Two budgets

In 2022, Jordan Hoffmann and colleagues at DeepMind investigated how to spend a fixed amount of training computation. Their Chinchilla model used fewer parameters and more training data than the larger Gopher model, with the same total training compute. The researchers reported stronger performance across a range of evaluations. The study challenged the idea that making the model larger was the best use of the available resource.[^chapter_07-c7-8]

The concrete choice makes the word budget useful. A research team has access to computing for a period. It can spend that resource on different combinations of model size, training duration, and data. Experimenting with those choices can reveal a better recipe. The factory supplies a constraint; it does not dictate a single design.

The Chinchilla work also involved multiple smaller training experiments. This is a case of comparison produced inside a large organization, directed at a particular technical problem. It is evidence against an argument that scale must always suppress experimentation. Resources can make demanding comparisons possible.

Now distinguish the computing budget from the comparison budget. The first concerns resources used to perform computation. The second, in my allocation model, adds the chances that successive opportunities go to someone outside the current favorite. They are different objects. A very large computing budget could support useful rival tests, or mainly serve a product already selected for deployment.

Spending on competing training recipes produces different evidence from spending on repeated deployment of a fixed recipe. Both can be productive. Only observations relevant to a defined comparison belong in an account of what the spending establishes about that comparison.

Neither description can be inferred from the electricity meter. The meter tells us about energy use. To understand the evidence, we need the designs, measurements, and decisions attached to it. A low electricity bill can accompany an informative test; an enormous bill can accompany repetition. The economic value of either depends on what is being accomplished.

Chinchilla also changed how its settings were adjusted, how text was divided into units, and the proportions drawn from different parts of the training dataset. The paper records those differences. Its result therefore compares two training packages; it does not isolate model size as the sole cause. The productive lesson is the researchers' ability to investigate a prevailing recipe. Future data, hardware, and uses could make another recipe preferable.[^chapter_07-c7-8]

Tests conducted before an award can reveal differences the award history alone leaves unresolved. Their measurements add to the evidence available about competing services.

Where deployment mainly reproduces the record of an established service, the questions concern how alternatives obtain use, which outcomes are checked, and whether tasks are comparable. A market share cannot answer those questions.

A million generated answers can reveal performance on encountered tasks without supplying the missing comparison. Applying Chapter 3's result requires specifying the observation process and counting every additional informative outcome and independent test.

Computing can make an established system more capable or make a competing design testable. Chinchilla demonstrates the productive value of varying the recipe. A buyer's pilot can test a different uncertainty about practical performance. The economic choice is how much capacity to devote to current production and how much to learning which approach is worth expanding.

## The five-million-dollar number

DeepSeek's V3 report contains a number likely to attract anyone interested in the economics of the intelligence factory: approximately 5.6 million dollars. It is the report's calculated cost for specified training work, using an assumed rental price of two dollars per H800 GPU hour. The authors explicitly exclude earlier research and experiments on architectures, algorithms, and data from that total.[^chapter_07-c7-6]

The qualification is part of the number's meaning. It does not turn an achievement into a failure. Producing an effective training run with fewer resources can be important. But a reader asking what it costs to create a capable AI organization needs a different account from a reader asking about the computing attributed to one completed training pipeline.

The distinction resembles the difference between paying for a successful reactor run and financing the attempts needed to develop the reactor. The earlier LED chapter showed how repeated modifications could make later production possible. An efficient final operation can rest on costly discovery. Measuring the operation accurately does not make those earlier costs vanish.

Published methods can reduce the cost of reproducing a successful approach. That is one benefit of knowledge transmission, and one reason reproduction cost differs from discovery cost.

Incomplete methods, unavailable data, and different operating conditions can still require fresh experiments. An expenditure figure in a research report does not, by itself, establish the cost at which another organization can reproduce the capability.

A company may spend heavily on research that never improves its final product. Later commercial success cannot show which of those expenses were necessary. Comparing development routes can help distinguish indispensable work from costly attempts that added little.

Kerr, Nanda, and Rhodes-Kropf's account of entrepreneurship supplies the established economic starting point. Early tests can inform later commitments, and the option to stop can matter. Bringing that account into AI asks us to examine the sequence of experiments and financing decisions. It does not give the final winner exclusive ownership of the concept of experimentation.[^chapter_07-c7-10]

DeepSeek's table covers a specified part of the work. Comparing its figure with another budget requires matching the activities included and the way computing time is valued. The cost of one completed training pipeline and the cost of developing the organization that produced it answer different questions.

## What can be copied

There is an asymmetry inside the factory. A trained model's numerical settings can be distributed without building an additional power station for each copy. Running the copied model still requires suitable equipment. Reproducing all the research that led to it is a further undertaking. Sharing the artifact, sharing access to computing, and sharing the means of discovery are different actions.

Kenneth Arrow's 1962 analysis of invention explains an older version of this difficulty. Knowledge can be costly and uncertain to produce while being relatively cheap to use or transmit once available. The social benefits of using it can exceed the rewards the producer can capture. Arrangements that create incentives to invent can also restrict its subsequent use.[^chapter_07-c7-11]

This is an established economic problem, not a new property discovered when AI appeared. What changes are the particular artifacts, costs, and control points. A model's settings might be available while its training data are not. A technical paper might describe a method while the equipment needed for a competitive implementation remains expensive. Different forms of access answer different constraints.

A common released model can support different services, customer problems, and error-reduction methods where its terms permit. Shared inputs can lower the cost of useful experiments without making their results redundant.

Suppose instead that our question concerns the training process itself: would another dataset or architecture have produced a better model? Two applications using unchanged copies of one model do not perform that comparison merely by having different company names. They may answer important application questions while leaving the training question unresolved.

That is why independence needs an object. Independence of ownership, computing provider, model ancestry, task assignment, or evaluation can each matter differently. Demanding independence in every respect could be prohibitively expensive. Assuming it exists because a storefront has a new name could be equally misleading.

Arrow's appropriation problem applies here. If immediate unrestricted access prevents recovery of the costs of valuable research, some research may never be undertaken. A route for later experiments is valuable only if the knowledge and equipment those experiments need also get produced.

The incentive argument still requires a comparison of feasible arrangements. Terms permitting returns and downstream experiments can differ from terms closing a customer route without enabling additional research. Both can be described as protecting investment while having different consequences.

Public support, contractual access, released artifacts, and competing private facilities all require resources. Each can make some experiments feasible and others expensive. The useful choice depends on the research or service being developed, including the cost of creating its inputs.

The terms attached to a model, a dataset, or a computing service help determine which later experiments are feasible. A release can make a new application possible while leaving another training strategy beyond reach. The factory creates possibilities. Its institutional arrangements determine who can act on them.

## From computing access to a useful comparison

Suppose a team has a different model design and enough money for a useful small trial. It still needs suitable computing, permission to connect its system to the intended service, and access to tasks whose results a customer will accept. If the only provider of an indispensable route refuses that use, the team can fail before its model produces a result worth comparing. The missing company is then absent from the market-share table as well as the performance table.

That absence can be structural. The resources needed to get started may depend on customers who will arrive only after the entrant reaches a scale it cannot finance. A new entrant could be viable once established while lacking a feasible path to establishment under the current rules. Interoperability, shared infrastructure, or different financing can change that path. The question is which change enables a credible attempt at a worthwhile cost.

AI scaling laws and this institutional problem concern different relationships. Evidence that a larger training run improves a measured capability does not establish that independent entry is impossible. The stronger concern arises when scale advantages combine with control over the resources, distribution, and evaluation needed to challenge them. An incumbent can then reinvest returns while rivals cannot reach the stage at which their work would generate useful evidence.

The duration of that advantage matters. A return protected by a development bottleneck can continue after an initial research investment has paid off. Its persistence may reflect continued invention, control of the starting conditions for competitors, or both. To understand the income, follow what the next investment enables and whose experiment the access rules prevent.

Computing access is one stage in producing evidence. A system must also reach relevant tasks, return interpretable outcomes, and permit those outcomes to affect a decision. These requirements connect infrastructure to discovery.

Task selection, comparable difficulty, valid outcome measures, privacy, and operational risk determine whether a service comparison is useful. The evaluation design has to justify its cost.

Distribution can close the route even when computing is available. A default that receives nearly every request can accumulate a record while alternatives obtain little ordinary-use evidence. Availability of an input does not guarantee access to an informative use.

Bell's patents and search distribution expose the same gap. A firm can possess a useful input while lacking a practical route to a customer. Computing for sale does little for an entrant barred from the tasks through which its service could be evaluated.

Performance can nevertheless justify a strong default. An investigation must include measured quality, actual switching, available comparisons, and the costs of alternatives. Access-dependent evidence is something to interpret carefully, rather than to dismiss.

Limited, well-measured comparisons can sometimes retain the convenience of a default while supplying useful evidence. Their value depends on the uncertainty resolved, implementation costs, and the decisions they can change.

The comparison budget prescribes no universal spending ratio. Small persistent chances can accumulate indefinitely, and large nominal opportunities can fail to identify a relevant effect. Evaluation concerns the process and its information.

Discovery also needs a route to action. A demonstration has less practical value if its result cannot influence subsequent adoption. Switching terms and interoperability help determine whether learning can change an economic arrangement.

An alternative training strategy needs capacity and permission to use it. An alternative service needs tasks and credible outcome checks. A buyer needs evidence relevant to the purchase. Several firms can depend on the same provider at one of those stages while remaining independent at another.

Nor does having many firms guarantee that all three are open. Several services could depend on the same model and the same route to users. They could still innovate meaningfully in their own domains. The useful question is what uncertainty their differences can resolve, and what important uncertainty remains outside their experiments.

Taking counterfactual economies seriously requires specifying feasible alternatives, what their tests could establish, and the full cost of producing that evidence. The resulting comparison can justify preserving a route without treating every proposed competitor as entitled to infrastructure.

## Who pays for the connection

The grid now returns to the story. On October 14, 2026, the IEA published another report on integrating data centers into electricity systems. It described connection queues containing duplicate or speculative proposals. A large queue can therefore present planners with both an urgent problem and an uncertain measure of how much demand will materialize.[^chapter_07-c7-12]

Imagine, solely as an illustration, one developer exploring sites on several grids while intending to build only one facility. Adding every request as if it represented a separate, certain future load would exaggerate the developer's eventual use. Ignoring the requests entirely could leave the winning location unprepared. The planner needs evidence about commitments and timing, rather than faith in a single headline total.

Here is another economic record that does not explain itself. A proposed project, financed construction, installed computing equipment, and sustained customer demand are different stages. Moving from one to the next can change the risks facing everyone involved. Treating a proposal as completed capacity confuses a possible future with an observed one.

The IEA's October report also describes the importance of allocating connection costs and risks. If new infrastructure is built for a large customer that consumes less than expected or leaves early, somebody must absorb the cost. Contractual commitments and tariffs can determine how much falls on other users of the electricity system. These are institutional choices alongside the engineering.

The IEA's comparison gives AEP Ohio's large-data-center arrangements a concrete form: a ramp-up period, minimum capacity payments, a long commitment, and exit fees. The customer reserving infrastructure cannot assume its obligation ends whenever its appetite for computing falls. That shifts some risk of unused capacity toward the customer requesting it. The terms are a way to allocate risk, not proof that the resulting investment is optimal or that households bear no remaining costs.[^chapter_07-c7-12]

Operating commitments matter as well as construction commitments. In another illustrative arrangement, a customer could accept occasional interruptions in exchange for earlier or cheaper access. A computing job able to pause safely might fit that bargain; a service promising immediate responses might find it costly. The relevant unit is the particular workload and obligation, rather than an undifferentiated category called AI demand.

That choice connects the contract back to the engineers. Recovery procedures, stored progress, and scheduling can change which interruptions are tolerable. A physical improvement might make a more flexible arrangement feasible; an inflexible promise to customers might rule it out. The institution and the machine have to be understood together.

We need evidence to distinguish those cases. A large electricity demand is not inherently a social loss. Nor does calling the demand innovative establish that every associated cost ought to be paid by others. The public question concerns useful output, displaced uses, environmental effects, and the allocation of risk as well as the private business plan.

A pilot using scarce capacity competes with other uses of the machines. What it might teach has to be worth the electricity, equipment, labor, and service forgone. A smaller test or a suitable existing record may answer the question at lower cost.

Long commitments can finance investment while restricting access to scarce capacity. A different arrangement could retain the financing benefit and preserve useful independent experiments. Whether it improves on the commitment depends on the experiments' value and the costs of making room for them.

This is the tension that a price or concentration statistic alone cannot resolve. A low price today can coexist with a poor route to testing tomorrow's alternative. A large producer can supply the very capacity that makes new experiments affordable. The economic task is to trace the mechanisms rather than assign one moral meaning to size.

## Leaving the factory

A working facility has overcome physical obstacles: pipes that could fail, interrupted training runs, equipment shortages, and difficult design choices. Those achievements make the next request possible.

But the completed training run is a beginning for the economic story. A model can be served repeatedly, incorporated into different products, evaluated on changing tasks, and routed toward some users rather than others. Those arrangements affect which observations accumulate and which alternatives develop a usable record.

Follow the next request. Someone chooses a system, the machines produce an answer, and a person or another program uses it. Who checks the answer? Can the buyer try another system on a comparable task? Which failures remain invisible if every request follows the same route?

One answer becomes a million, then a billion. Each can be useful. How many of them tell us whether a different system would work better?

---

<a id="chapter_08"></a>

# Chapter 8

## A billion answers

Inference turns a trained model into a stream of outputs. Each interaction can also create evidence, but what it reveals depends on which requests arrive, how the model is selected, and whether the answer is checked.

The records can include the request, selected model, answer, time taken, and a user's acceptance. A check against an independently known outcome adds another observation. These records answer different questions because they arise from different measurement choices.

The trained model need not change its numerical settings during this exchange. A later development process might use collected examples to update it. The service could also retrieve a newer document or apply a different instruction without retraining the underlying model. Improvement can occur in the model, the surrounding software, or the people evaluating the result.

Now repeat the exchange a billion times across many workplaces and tasks. The records can become immensely useful. They can expose recurring mistakes, reveal delays, and help developers improve a service. But the number of completed requests does not, by itself, tell us which questions the resulting evidence can answer.

One question concerns this particular order: did the service find the correct deadline? Another concerns the alternative model: would it have done better on comparable work? A third concerns the development process: which investment produced the capability? A fourth concerns an economic arrangement: what would have happened if another supplier had received the early opportunities and resources to develop?

We can learn a great deal about the first question without settling all the others. We can also design the service to collect evidence about more than one. The choice matters before the billionth answer arrives. It matters when we decide what happens to the first request.

## Two answers to one question

Chatbot Arena offers a concrete departure from an ordinary exchange. In the platform described by Wei-Lin Chiang and colleagues in their 2024 paper, a user enters a prompt and receives responses from two anonymously selected models. The user compares them and votes; the identities are revealed afterward. The interaction creates a paired preference observation, rather than simply another conversation with a named favorite.[^chapter_08-c8-1]

The two columns on the screen do useful work. They bring alternatives into the same encounter, expose them to the same prompt, and delay the information that a famous brand might provide. We can argue about the design while recognizing what it adds. A record of which service people already use supplies a different kind of evidence from a vote after they have seen two responses.

Preference and correctness remain different. A polished wrong answer can be more convincing than a correct but awkwardly expressed one. Assessing accuracy may require evidence beyond the preference judgment.

Arena's paper itself distinguishes benchmarks based on human preference from those based on a known correct answer. That distinction helps us resist treating one ranking as a certificate for every use. A buyer interested in pleasant conversation and a buyer extracting deadlines may need different measurements. Neither has to dismiss the other's question to insist on measuring its own.

Prompts and users arriving at a public platform can differ from those in a specialized application. Long documents, damaged scans, or technical vocabulary can change performance. A general benchmark is evidence to assess for transportability, rather than an entire decision basis.

Unequal task assignments complicate performance comparisons. A service handling clear documents can have a higher acceptance rate than one handling damaged scans even if its underlying capability is no better. Evaluation must examine the assignment process as well as the score.

A paired test can help with that particular problem. We could send suitable examples from each category to both services and assess their outputs using a consistent procedure. The cost includes additional computation and evaluation. The benefit is evidence directed toward a decision that a single unbalanced stream would have made harder.

The Arena design also reminds us that comparison has to be produced. Someone builds the interface, selects model pairs, collects responses, and maintains the analysis. Those activities are part of the institution. The platform's methodological choices affect what a vote means, which differences it can reveal, and how confidently a ranking can be interpreted.

Suppose a purchasing department then uses a ranking to select its default supplier. The ranking can influence subsequent exposure. That possibility does not invalidate the original tests. It creates a new question about how the tests and the resulting deployment interact. A good measured result can become an input into a process that later generates a different kind of evidence.

Service and evaluation can be designed together. Delivering an accurate answer at an acceptable cost remains the productive aim; a suitable comparison can reveal when another system better serves it.

## When the judge likes the packaging

Yann Dubois and colleagues tested how an automatic judge responds to presentation in their length-controlled AlpacaEval study. They prompted models to give concise or verbose answers. For one GPT-4 version, the unadjusted win rate ranged from about twenty-three to sixty-four percent as the instruction changed. The underlying model had not received a new training run; the prompt and resulting answers differed. Their statistical length adjustment reduced the swing. The experiment exposes sensitivity to presentation, while leaving us to investigate which changes also improve the answer.[^chapter_08-c8-12]

Each response faced a reference answer to the same instruction. The judge's preference supplied the score. It was a measure of which answer the evaluator favored, rather than an independent check against a separately established correct solution.[^chapter_08-c8-12]

Arena records users' comparisons of two answers. AlpacaEval uses the judgment of a particular automatic evaluator. An adjustment to that evaluator's scores tells us about its measurements; human voting needs evidence of its own.

Think of the deadline in the purchase order. One service returns the date and identifies the relevant passage. Another returns the same date inside a long explanation of the document. A buyer might prefer either presentation for a legitimate reason. An evaluation intended to measure the extraction task should nevertheless understand how much of its ranking depends on the surrounding prose.

Now imagine that the longer response contains an error elsewhere. The extra detail has changed more than the length of the answer; it has created another opportunity to mislead the worker. Alternatively, the longer explanation might expose an ambiguity that the short answer concealed. Length by itself cannot tell us which happened. The measurement must remain connected to the purpose.

The length-controlled study also makes a counterfactual question explicit: how would the estimated preference compare if the responses had the same length? Its statistical adjustment supplies an estimate under a model. The authors are not claiming to have observed every possible rewritten answer or eliminated every source of evaluation bias. A defined counterfactual can be useful without becoming an omniscient reconstruction.

For a developer, the practical stakes concern which improvements an evaluation rewards. If a score rises after a formatting change, the developer should investigate whether users actually complete their work better. If better reasoning raises the score, that is worth distinguishing too. The same final ranking can emerge through changes with different consequences for the customer.

Who defines the test helps determine who receives the next opportunity. An accessible benchmark can support discovery, but its convenience is insufficient: its design must expose differences relevant to the intended use.

## Choosing before paying

The next design decision concerns which models to call. Sending every request to the most expensive available system may waste resources on tasks a cheaper system handles adequately. Calling every model for every request can produce comparisons, but also an unnecessary bill. The problem invites a selector: software that routes the request according to the work it appears to require.

Isaac Ong and colleagues' RouteLLM research investigates such a selector. Their framework learns from preference data and routes between a stronger, more expensive model and a cheaper alternative. They evaluate the resulting tradeoffs on specified benchmarks and compare their methods with random routing. Their reported improvements concern those models, data, and evaluations, rather than a guarantee for every future workplace.[^chapter_08-c8-2]

The purchase order makes the motivation easy to understand. A short document with a clearly labeled date may require less capability than an ambiguous revision spread across several pages. A well-designed router could reserve the more costly service for the cases where it adds enough value. That is a productive improvement even if the user never sees the selection happening.

A router needs evidence about which model performs well on each kind of request. The strongest overall model may offer little advantage on a simple task. Paying for it on every request can waste resources a task-specific choice would save.

The RouteLLM paper also reports limits to transferring a router across tasks. Data resembling the eventual evaluation can improve performance. Correspondence among training examples, evaluation cases, and actual use therefore matters to the interpretation of a score.

Lingjiao Chen, Matei Zaharia, and James Zou explore a related route in FrugalGPT. Their system can call a model, assess the response, and decide whether to stop or call another. The answer already obtained becomes an input into the next decision. The study compares cost and performance on defined tasks, including classification and reading comprehension.[^chapter_08-c8-3]

In the workplace illustration, the system might first ask a cheaper model to extract the deadline. If its assessment of that response is unfavorable, it could ask another model. This differs from selecting once before seeing an answer. It also differs from obtaining an independent human check of correctness. The assessment mechanism itself needs evaluation.

FrugalGPT's paper includes cases where additional calls are made even though the models' answers agree. Its discussion identifies the selector's uncertainty as a source of avoidable expense. Delegating a decision to a model leaves another judgment to evaluate: when does that model decide to stop?

An economical cascade might lower the cost of trying alternative services. If the interface reports only one answer and one brand, the buyer may be unable to inspect those comparisons. Several logos can conceal dependence on one underlying service; a simple interface can conceal useful tests of several.

Routing and cascades can make it cheaper to compare services as well as to use them. A buyer who can afford only one expensive answer may be able to check several cheaper ones. Whether that happens depends on what the selection system tests and records.

One distinction is essential before we try to measure a comparison budget. A router's estimate that one model will produce the preferred answer differs from the chance that the request will actually be assigned to that model. RouteLLM uses a predicted preference and a threshold to make its selection. The prediction and the assignment rule do different jobs.

A predicted seventy percent chance of producing the preferred answer is not a seventy percent allocation probability. A selector could send every request to the predicted winner. Adding the remaining thirty percent figures would then mismeasure the process that actually assigns work.

A different rule could deliberately randomize some assignments. Its allocation probabilities would describe that design, while its predicted performance scores would describe the selector's expectations. Both can be recorded. Their relationship matters to the analysis, but they are not interchangeable numbers simply because both can be expressed as percentages.

Checking answers reveals performance on the tasks a system encounters; independent evaluation can supply information about alternatives. Those measurements belong alongside the assignment record when we assess what can be learned.

The work of checking also has a production process. Someone may need to open an amendment, resolve an ambiguous field, or determine which document version governs the task. AI could assist with that work, but an automated score remains something to validate against the relevant standard. A service can lower the cost of producing an answer while leaving costly uncertainty about whether the answer is useful.

The relevant chain runs from the observations training a selector through assignment rules, checked responses, and decisions using those checks. Following it distinguishes efficient production from useful comparison and unsupported precision.

The objectives still need to be named. A router optimized for the best current cost/performance tradeoff need not be optimized to discover a new supplier's potential. It may already have enough evidence for its operational decision, or it may need occasional new observations. We cannot determine which merely by noting that the routing is automated.

## A record worth reusing

Long before today's language models, a news page posed a related problem. The Yahoo Today Module could highlight an article for a visitor and observe whether the visitor clicked. It could not observe that same visit's response to every article left unshown. Lihong Li, Wei Chu, John Langford, and Xuanhui Wang investigated how previously collected data could help evaluate another recommendation rule.[^chapter_08-c8-4]

Their work used records gathered through randomized article selection. Under their stated conditions, an evaluator can retain suitable events where a proposed rule would have selected the article that was actually shown. The retained record then includes a real response to that choice. The researchers compared offline evaluations with online results in the Yahoo application.

A historical record can contain deliberately created variation. It can then support questions that simply repeating the current favorite would leave unanswered. Collecting the right record expands what later analysis can discover.

Randomized request logs with checked outcomes can support evaluation of another selection rule under the relevant coverage and identification assumptions. A literal parallel economy is not required for every counterfactual question.

That last qualification matters. If a service was never available for a particular kind of order, the record may contain no relevant responses from it. Knowing that the probability was zero does not supply the missing performance. An estimator needs suitable observations or additional defensible assumptions, rather than an increasingly elaborate way of describing their absence.

The Yahoo authors call their method replay. Their use of the word concerns evaluating a recommendation policy with suitable logged events. My shadow futures concern alternative histories generated under specified economic conditions and rules. These are distinct uses. A procedure that reuses article records is not a time machine for recreating the entire industry with a different first winner.

Evaluation of current systems, variation across alternative development histories, and the effects of changing a development policy require different comparisons. Randomized request logs can help with the first without establishing how capabilities would have evolved after different early opportunities.

A record can justify its collection by helping a buyer choose a better service. That decision can be worthwhile even when the evidence leaves each supplier's past contribution unresolved.

This gives us a practical reason to preserve details that a success count omits. Which alternatives were available? Why was this one selected? What was measured afterward? A usable assignment record and meaningful outcome can make a small pilot worth more, for a specific decision, than a much larger archive lacking those features.

The pilot also has costs. Someone receives the less suitable answer when the comparison goes badly. Someone checks the outcome. Some data may be inappropriate to retain or share. Useful evaluation requires an observation design that fits the task and evidence worth those costs.

## The same ancestor

Our hypothetical buyer opens three services and receives three answers to the order question. Three storefronts are visible. What has been compared?

Perhaps each service has independently trained a different system. Perhaps each has adapted a shared model. Perhaps all three pass the request to the same supplier, with different surrounding instructions. The answers may still differ. The number of interfaces does not identify which part of the production process varied.

Meta's Llama 3 report supplies a concrete example of a relationship between models. It describes using its flagship model to improve smaller models during post-training. Different members of a model family can therefore be connected through the development process. Treating their outputs as evidence from wholly unrelated research histories would miss that connection.[^chapter_08-c8-5]

A smaller model can behave differently, require fewer resources, and fit a particular application better. Testing it against its larger relative can guide the purchase. Both can still inherit a training assumption the comparison never varies.

Imagine that our three services extract the same wrong date because the underlying document representation loses the same footnote. Asking all three may produce reassuring agreement while leaving that error untouched. Now imagine that one service uses a better document parser and finds the footnote. A common language model has not prevented a meaningful improvement elsewhere in the service.

These are illustrative cases, but they identify an empirical task. Follow the chain far enough to understand which components are common, which vary, and where the outcome is measured. Independence is valuable relative to a question. It should not be a ceremonial word attached to a company name.

A thousand carefully checked orders can expose failure patterns that one order could not reveal. New requests and new checks can add information about a system even when the same model handles them all.

But generating a thousand responses without a new check does not create a thousand independent confirmations of the underlying claim. If the uncertainty concerns the actual delivery date, the order and its amendments remain relevant evidence. A longer conversation among programs might help interpret them. It cannot make an unread amendment irrelevant by agreeing without it.

Yaneer Bar-Yam's “From Big Data to Important Information” distinguishes detailed records from information useful for understanding an intervention.[^chapter_08-c8-6] For the delivery order, the decisive detail may be an amendment that thousands of generated answers have never checked.

The workplace can act on that warning. It can measure whether the extracted date is correct, whether the answer helped a worker complete the task, and whether a different service changes the result. Each measurement has a purpose. We gain clarity by naming those purposes before admiring the size of the resulting database.

## Predicting the next choice

Forecasts of the next allocation can converge while explanations of productive contribution remain different. Chapter 3 states the conditions under which that distinction persists.

David Blackwell and Lester Dubins showed in 1962 how conditional forecasts can merge under an appropriate relationship between probability laws. Hao Zhang later established an example in spatial statistics where some underlying parameters cannot be consistently recovered even though important prediction can remain effective.[^chapter_08-c8-7][^chapter_08-c8-8]

Forecasting which supplier wins an allocation is a different task from checking whether an AI answer is true. The answer needs evidence of its own.

Acceptance, correctness, and causal contribution are separate targets. Predicting that a user accepts an answer does not establish that it is accurate, or identify which investment caused the capability. A common interface does not make the underlying inference identical.

For the buyer, the next action could be straightforward. If errors matter, check a suitable sample against the original orders. If supplier choice matters, preserve a meaningful comparison. If a historical attribution claim matters, identify the intervention, horizon, and evidence that could support it. The inquiry becomes more useful as it becomes more specific.

## Sixteen agents and one compiler

A swarm sounds like a crowd. On a computer, it can mean several copies of a model pursuing assigned work, using tools, keeping records, and returning results. The economic question begins with what those activities accomplish. Sixteen running programs can perform sixteen useful investigations, or spend the afternoon rediscovering one mistake.

In February 2026, Nicholas Carlini at Anthropic reported setting sixteen Claude instances to build a C compiler, which translates code into instructions a computer can run. Compiling the Linux operating system, they stalled on the same bug and interfered with one another's fixes. He redesigned the tests: GCC, an established compiler, handled most files while the new compiler handled varying subsets. Failures became easier to isolate. The reported achievement still had limits, including inefficient generated code and dependence on other tools. This was an attributed capability experiment, not a measured replacement for a human workforce.[^chapter_08-c8-agents]

The revised tests helped separate failures. The checking arrangement belonged to the productive machinery as surely as the model did. A candidate program needed reliable tests that could expose its mistakes.

There are different ways to organize that machinery. Anthropic's June 2025 research system used a lead agent to distribute searches among others. Carlini's compiler account explicitly described a setup without a lead agent assigning tasks. In either arrangement, ask who directs the work and supplies the feedback.[^chapter_08-c8-agents]

Agent systems can organize candidate generation, execution, and checking, as the compiler account demonstrates. Their productive value depends on the full arrangement: human-defined objectives, tools, tests, and judgments about errors, as well as the cost of model calls.

Retained code, test cases, and usable project records can support later comparison when a model changes. The rights to those assets therefore affect the cost of developing and evaluating another approach.

Failed attempts can matter as much as final outputs. Preserving their records may prevent repeated mistakes, while requiring storage, documentation, and protection of confidential material.

Control of tools and records can make switching expensive even when software generation becomes cheap. This is an institutional question about portability and ownership, distinct from the capability demonstrated by an agent experiment.

Carlini's account describes checks within one firm using related models. A legally independent project could still use a defective test. Validity depends on feedback that can expose an error, wherever the test is organized.

A thousand agents can reproduce one mistaken interpretation; a single check against a relevant document can reveal it. Abundant software labor can multiply attempts faster than it produces trustworthy judgments of their results.

## A town throws a party

In a 2023 study, Joon Sung Park and colleagues populated Smallville with twenty-five software agents. One received the intention to organize a Valentine's Day party. The authors report invitations spreading and five invited agents arriving at the cafe. Another resident mistakenly identified her neighbor, Adam Smith, as the author of The Wealth of Nations. Knowledge inherited from a language model had intruded into the neighborhood.[^chapter_08-c8-10]

These characters supply a tempting contrast with a compiler. A simulated town can be rerun, with rules changed and histories recorded. But what would establish that its response to a tax change predicts the response of people? Believable conversation and a program's successful execution do not supply that validation. The study investigated believability and agent architecture, not the accuracy of economic policy forecasts.

Simulated demand can reveal implications of assumptions and help design an empirical test. Its preferences may also reflect the instructions and training inherited from a common model. Multiplying simulated participants reduces some sampling noise without correcting a mistaken demand mechanism.

A shadow replay keeps underlying parameters, starting conditions, and response rules fixed while the random history changes. Altering procurement or training policy is a separate intervention. Either simulation needs evidence connecting its rules to the actual economy. A generated conversation can reveal how programmed agents interact. An executed program with independent checks can also expose a proposed design's errors.

## When a generated answer can be tested

There is a more encouraging route from generation to evidence when a proposed answer can be checked directly. Google DeepMind's 2025 AlphaEvolve report describes a system that proposes computer programs, runs evaluations, and uses the results to guide further proposals. The language model helps generate candidates; executable tests supply feedback about what those candidates do.[^chapter_08-c8-13]

One application concerned scheduling jobs in Google's data centers. A machine can have spare computing capacity but be unable to take another job because another resource, such as memory, is already exhausted. The report describes searching for a better rule for assigning work to available machines. This is the factory's coordination problem turned into an object of automated investigation.

The researchers used a simulator based on historical workloads, tested on unseen recent data, and then measured operation after deployment. They reported recovering an average of 0.7 percent of fleet-wide computing resources that would otherwise have been stranded. That measures available computing capacity, rather than an equivalent reduction in electricity use.

The sequence matters as much as the headline. A proposal entered a test with a defined outcome. A promising simulated result faced another check. Deployment supplied further observations. The program was not considered better simply because another language model produced a fluent explanation of why it ought to work.

Smallville's party unfolds under programmed rules. A scheduling simulation can also misrepresent a real system. In the AlphaEvolve account, measurements from subsequent operation provided a way to check the simulated gains against actual performance.

For software generation, useful evidence comes from executed programs and checked outcomes. Candidate code can help organize a search, but its correctness needs testing beyond the model's ability to produce it.

The test set still needs attention. A program might exploit a quirk of the examples, perform well on clear documents, and fail on revisions. A good development process would investigate such limits. Automating the proposals makes the quality of evaluation more consequential because many candidates can now be optimized against whatever test we provide.

This possibility strengthens the constructive part of the comparison argument. An AI system can help us propose and assess alternatives at a cost that changes which experiments are worth attempting. It can do so inside a large firm or in a smaller undertaking. The organizational label does not tell us whether the tests are informative.

Evidence that a scheduling rule uses machines better can justify adopting that rule. The experiment compares ways to schedule the available machines. It leaves the contributions of earlier investors and the effects of alternative ownership arrangements to other evidence.

AI can lower the cost of producing a proposal and, where independent checks work, the cost of finding out whether it succeeds. That expands the practical room for discovery. When every proposal instead inherits the same untested assumption, multiplying outputs leaves that assumption in place. The decisive resource is the route from a candidate answer to a result capable of overturning it.

## What the billion requests leave behind

Record design determines what inference can follow. A final answer and acceptance signal supply different evidence from a record also containing task characteristics, assignment rules, checked outcomes, and appropriate alternative-system tests. Equal request volumes need not produce equally informative records.

Acceptance can reflect verification, difficulty checking, or time pressure. Comparing outcomes with independently checked evidence helps distinguish those explanations and identify specific failures. The feedback signal must be interpreted before it is treated as correctness.

Production can be concentrated while a limited experiment preserves a relevant comparison. Assignments justified as learning must expose informative differences and be capable of affecting a decision, rather than merely adding a second name to a record.

The favorite can vary by task. Aggregate request totals can conceal a lack of within-task comparison, while a dominant service can host useful internal experiments. Logged assignment rules and checked outcomes matter together.

Adding agents can multiply work without giving another supplier a chance at a task. The comparison budget accumulates the conditional chances of allocation outside the current favorite, given the history so far. Checks on the team's completed work supply further evidence about its performance. Both the assignments and those checked outcomes affect what can be learned.

Stanford's 2026 AI Index distinguishes training from inference in discussing energy use. Each extra attempt still requires computing after the model has been developed. More attempts are worthwhile when what they produce or reveal is worth those resources.[^chapter_08-c8-11]

AI can lower the cost of generating and testing alternatives. It can also operate within institutions that concentrate control of records, distribution, and evaluation. Its useful output and its effect on independent discovery both belong in the economic assessment.

The constructive choice is to identify a valuable uncertainty and the observation that could reduce it. A paired vote, checked document, randomized assignment, reusable log, or independent deployment can help under suitable conditions. Testing today's extractor also differs from testing whether its use teaches workers to recognize tomorrow's difficult cases. The next question follows the person who has to do that work.

## The person at the other end

A date in a report depends on more than the model that supplied it. The computing, service selection, evaluation, and person checking the answer all help determine whether the date can be trusted and used.

Someone supplied examples used in development. Someone expressed a preference in an evaluation, prepared a document, checked an answer, or corrected an error. Arena's paired votes make one such contribution visible. In a workplace, the corresponding judgments can be less conspicuous, folded into the ordinary activity of getting a task done.

If an AI service improves the worker's performance, that is a real possibility worth measuring. If it changes which tasks the worker gets to attempt, how expertise develops, or what an employer can observe about ability, those are further questions. An answer that helps finish today's task can also change the conditions under which tomorrow's worker learns.

Behind the answers are people doing work, people whose work is changing, and beginners still waiting for a first assignment. The system that handles today's task also helps determine who gets to learn tomorrow's job.

---

<a id="chapter_09"></a>

# Chapter 9

## The first rung

On September 10, 2020, an engineer ran a load test and an AI assistant stopped making suggestions. The interruption appears in an unusual place: an economics paper about customer support. Workers continued answering customers while the software that had been helping them was unavailable. A breakdown had created an opportunity to ask what remained after assistance disappeared.[^chapter_09-c9-1]

Usually, a company wants to know whether its new tool works. Does it resolve more complaints? Does it shorten the queue? Does the customer leave satisfied? An outage raises another question. Has the worker acquired something that can survive the disappearance of the machine?

A worker and a computer can become a more productive team without the worker becoming more capable alone. They can also become a more productive team because the worker learns from the computer. The difference is easy to miss while both are working.
