Workable capital income and inheritance taxation offer routes to financing broader participation; their investment and financing effects determine the terms. Uncertainty about contribution alone supplies neither a preferred instrument nor a tax rate.

The uses of the revenue should be concrete. A shared computing facility could let a qualified team attempt a different model. An independently governed laboratory could test failures that no supplier has an incentive to disclose. Grants could finance credible first demonstrations. Broadly held funds or employee ownership could distribute claims on productive assets beyond their existing owners. These are proposals for widening the means to attempt and benefit from economic activity; their performance has to be measured against their costs.

Public project funders should publish qualification rules, allow competing methods, and provide appeal against exclusion. A grant recipient should be able to report failure without concealing it to protect the program. Continuing project support should depend on what is learned and on the credible value of further work. Public finance should create alternatives to private gatekeeping while remaining answerable to independent judgment itself.

These fiscal purposes should remain visible in the accounts. A conduct-based charge seeks to change a harmful incentive. General taxation finances shared capabilities and broader participation. Pooling their revenues may be convenient, but financing a valuable laboratory does not establish that every contributor caused an equivalent informational loss. Nor should the same harm be charged twice through overlapping instruments.

Capital can finance the equipment, time, and risk that discovery requires. It can also accumulate around a route whose owner decides who else may enter. Capital income and large inheritances should help finance the shared capabilities through which other people can become productive owners. The terms must preserve reasons to invest. Leaving accumulated returns entirely outside that responsibility allows a successful position to pass to the next generation together with the power to prevent its challengers from developing.

## A dividend for the futures that never began

Imagine that a few AI and robotics companies come to supply much of society's productive capacity. Their systems work, their owners keep investing, and alternatives have no feasible route through the networks, customers, and evaluations needed to develop. Another year of successful production deepens the incumbents' record. It does not supply the missing histories of the challengers that never got started.

Everyone should enter economic life with a claim to participate in what it can become. Ownership should reward a company for what it builds while leaving other people room to build something else. When a few private systems become unavoidable gateways and close those routes, society should retain a share of the returns for everyone. Protecting investment should carry a reciprocal obligation to share the prosperity produced under an arrangement that narrows other people's practical choices.

That share should attach to membership in the society accepting those terms. It should reach people before an employer, investor, or platform decides whether their potential is worth backing. Making the payment depend on that approval would give the same institutions another power to decide who may participate in prosperity.

Who could prove what they would have become? A worker might never acquire the skills a first assignment would have taught. A business might never reach the customer who would have financed its next improvement. Requiring proof of those unrealized careers would let the missing history defeat the claim before it could be heard. A shared entitlement gives everyone a stake without asking an administrator to rank their imagined earnings.

The public gives up part of its capacity to develop and test alternatives when it accepts those closed routes. A universal social dividend should be part of what it receives in return. Its claim begins with that shared capacity, before anyone has to prove entrepreneurial promise. The payment would remain due to a person who chooses a different life entirely.

Thomas Paine made a related distinction about land. Private improvement could deserve protection while the community retained a claim for the natural inheritance absorbed by ownership. His proposed fund would make a payment at adulthood and provide an older-age income, with entitlement extending to rich and poor.[^chapter_11-c11-ubi-paine] Philippe Van Parijs later defended an unconditional basic income through real freedom and access to resources, extending his account to the rents attached to scarce jobs.[^chapter_11-c11-ubi-parijs] A universal claim on part of an economy's returns has an intellectual history. The loss of opportunities to develop and test alternatives gives that claim another economic basis.

A universal basic income puts that entitlement into practice through a regular, unconditional payment. Universality governs the payment; progressivity governs contributions to finance it. Capital income and inheritance taxation should help fund it, alongside returns on broadly held productive assets. People with large economic claims should contribute more to the settlement, even while receiving the same basic entitlement.

Alaska offers a practical reference for sharing returns. Its Permanent Fund receives mineral revenues, invests, and helps finance payments to eligible residents as well as public services.[^chapter_11-c11-ubi-alaska] An annual resource dividend is smaller in ambition than an adequate income floor, and natural resources differ from an AI company. The institution demonstrates that a cash claim can attach to membership rather than to a recipient's work for the enterprises producing the returns.

An income floor has to be financed from actual resources. Its level must account for durable revenues, investment, work incentives, and the public services people still need.

The recipient can use the payment to care for someone, study, refuse a bad bargain, or live with greater security. A grant finances a project that must justify its use of public resources. A dividend gives a person the freedom to choose a purpose without first persuading a funder of its commercial value.

The dividend must accompany access duties and independent routes to discovery. Paying people while leaving a closed gateway untouched would share some income while preserving the obstruction. A dividend is no license to close the next gate.

## Choosing rules before knowing every contribution

A defensible charge needs a causal chain: identified conduct, changed comparative evidence, affected decisions and beneficiaries, and an assessed loss after private responses and feasible replacement. It must then improve the attainable outcome after productive benefits, financing, enforcement, and entry are counted.

The time horizon must match on both sides. Software changes can make some findings obsolete while leaving enduring failure modes relevant. Longer effects on learning and entry require stated assumptions and evidence. A temporary information problem should not become a permanent fiscal category after its basis has changed.

Participation can change too. Qualification costs may deter useful entrants; predictable obligations may support investment. Warnings about reduced innovation need investigation, rather than automatic acceptance or dismissal. These are responses affecting the case for a particular instrument.

Access duties, payments for verified testing, public provision, and existing private arrangements have to be compared on those terms. Where the evidence cannot support a precise corrective rate, a narrower access remedy or a limited trial can establish what works. General financing choices can still proceed through an explicit public judgment about benefits, burdens, and incentives.

We do not need agreement about the exact share of every fortune attributable to its owner before choosing a tax system. We need agreement about what that system should make possible, how its costs will be borne, and how its results can be challenged. A reward record that cannot settle contribution should not be asked to certify the rules protecting its own distribution.

A settlement that rewards useful investment, broadens ownership, and finances independent routes to discovery gives enterprise a future beyond its current owners. A successful business can earn substantial returns while contributing to the resources through which another business might demonstrate a better way. The next task is to make those opportunities observable in the institutions economics studies.

---

<a id="chapter_12"></a>

# Chapter 12

## An economics of possible worlds

The winter wheat planted at Broadbalk in autumn 1843 was harvested in 1844. The records of that harvest were the beginning of a much longer undertaking. Fertilizers, manure, and untreated ground supplied contrasts that successive generations of researchers continued to study. The field produced grain. Keeping differences alive helped it produce evidence too.[^chapter_12-c12-1]

An ordinary commercial field might sensibly receive whatever treatment its owner expects to work best. Broadbalk had another purpose. Some ground continued receiving treatments whose value could be assessed against other ground. Giving every strip the apparently best treatment would have changed what the field could teach.

The field has two products: grain and evidence. Producing the second requires land, labor, records, and arrangements that preserve useful differences. The harvest serves one purpose; the knowledge can benefit people who never buy its wheat.

The long record also makes a difficulty visible. An experiment cannot remain useful simply by refusing to change. Weeds, soil acidity, new varieties, and changing farming practices can alter the question a comparison answers. Rothamsted's account of its experiments describes considered modifications alongside preserved treatment differences.[^chapter_12-c12-2]

By the late 1940s, some plots had become so acidic that comparing two nitrogen fertilizers no longer answered the intended question. In the separate Agdell experiment, acidity favored a disease that depressed turnip yields; the experiment required extensive modification in 1951. Researchers applied chalk to correct acidity. Broadbalk later received shorter-strawed wheat varieties and a revised cropping arrangement in 1968. Preserving a useful experiment required changing what happened on the ground.[^chapter_12-c12-2]

Repeated applications of manure and fertilizer altered the soils themselves, leaving different stores of nutrients and organic matter. Later investigations could study those reserves and their effects. Yesterday's treatment had become part of today's condition. A difference after many years can reflect the accumulated consequences of earlier management as well as the immediate application. Comparing this year's input, a sustained practice, or a change from the inherited state asks different questions.

A field trial compares treatments with accumulated histories. It cannot erase their earlier development. Likewise, evaluating available technologies and estimating how different early opportunities would have changed their development are different questions, with different evidence requirements.

There is no timeless instruction here to hold everything fixed. A researcher must know which difference is being maintained, what else has changed, and whether the resulting observation still bears on the intended question. A familiar treatment name cannot guarantee an unchanged experiment.

AI evaluation faces a similar continuity problem. An unchanged benchmark can become a precise answer to an obsolete question. Revising it only when a favored system performs badly can undermine comparability. Documented changes are part of the evidence.

Broadbalk's plan distinguishes cropping and management histories across treatment strips. The name alone does not identify the ground, treatment, or period producing an observation. The same discipline applies to software: a service label can survive changes in models, interfaces, and review procedures. Version history helps separate those changes from differences in assigned work.

The fields yield crops and measurements: harvests, soil conditions, and changes across treated plots. Maintaining those comparisons is part of Rothamsted's productive work. Keeping an experiment going can make questions answerable that an ordinary harvest would leave unresolved.

Economics studies how resources produce goods and services, how incentives influence behavior, and how uncertainty affects choices. The arrangements that let alternatives develop interpretable records deserve the same attention. Losing those arrangements changes what an economy can know about its own possibilities.

## A second use for the harvest

Rothamsted's archived samples can be examined with methods unavailable when they were collected. Physical material preserves possibilities that a table of old measurements alone cannot supply.[^chapter_12-c12-3]

Sarah Bearchell and colleagues powdered old leaves and stems using liquid nitrogen and extracted short DNA sequences, allowing for damage in the aged material. Their 2005 study examined two wheat pathogens in samples spanning 1844–2003. Gaps required combining material from two plots after checks of comparability. Changes in relative pathogen abundance were associated with sulfur-dioxide emissions. The researchers considered other correlated factors; atmospheric pollution had not been randomized.[^chapter_12-c12-4]

Someone had kept the samples before knowing that a future laboratory could ask them this question. The archive connected a new method to observations that could no longer be collected afresh. It could preserve material from a harvest that occurred. It could not supply the harvest from a treatment never applied to that ground.

Digital records have the same distinction. Requests, versions, outputs, corrections, and checked outcomes can support more questions than a success count. They cannot manufacture the response of an alternative that was never tried.

Preservation costs money and can expose sensitive information. A useful record retains the context needed for authorized questions, with limits on access and a reason for keeping it. Missing entries, forgotten selection rules, and changing definitions can make even a large archive hard to interpret. Keeping everything indiscriminately can burden people without helping a consequential decision.

The durable resource is the connection among the task, the choice, and what happened afterward. Later investigators can revisit that connection as their methods improve. Where the needed event never occurred, someone must create a new comparison.

## The arrangement makes a difference

Ronald Fisher's 1926 paper on field experiments began with a familiar difficulty: apparently similar ground can yield differently. Weighing the crop accurately does not establish how much a treatment caused the difference. The arrangement of the plots affects what the result means.[^chapter_12-c12-5]

Fisher examined how allocation and replication affect estimates of error. Appropriate random arrangements could support an inference that a systematic pattern of treatment labels could undermine. Broadbalk's original plots were not a modern randomized trial; its evidence comes from maintained contrasts, subsequent changes, and accumulated observations. Each record has to be understood through the process that made it.

A service comparison likewise needs tasks of known difficulty, suitable assignment, and trustworthy checks. A shared faulty reference can make several systems agree on a wrong answer. Repeating the evaluation does not repair that common error.

The comparison budget adds a specific quantity to this account of evidence: cumulative allocation chance outside the current favorite. Under the Chapter 3 conditions, it limits information about productive input effects in the allocation history. Customer outcomes, independent tests, or other informative measurements can supply evidence beyond that history.

An empirical application can examine a defined period without asserting that a market's lifetime budget is finite. Assignment probabilities need a documented basis and uncertainty where they are estimated. Completed-work shares cannot substitute for them. Paired performance measurements contain more information than a list of allocation winners, and the analysis must count it.

Simulation can test a method in an artificial market whose rules are known. Morris, White, and Crowther explain how explicit aims, data-generating mechanisms, and performance measures make such a study interpretable.[^chapter_12-c12-7] Connecting its findings to an actual economy requires evidence about that economy's rules. A successful recovery of an artificial parameter tests the method in that setting; a well-designed field comparison tests the real decision.

## What an empirical test would need

Kasy and Sautmann's agricultural recruitment application supplies a documented example of matching experimentation to policy choice. Six recruitment options were tested through an adaptive calling design with 10,000 numbers over seventeen waves. The outcome was completion of five recruitment questions, rather than later agricultural income.[^chapter_12-c12-9]

The call experiment measured recruitment. To assess the agricultural service's effects on yields or income, researchers would need to follow those outcomes as well. What gets recorded determines which decisions the evidence can support.

A useful study would distinguish assignment chances, observed outcomes, and the effect being estimated. It would ask what can be learned from allocations alone, how checked outcomes change the answer, and how sensitive the conclusion is to plausible feedback rules.

Repeated records from one event, common shocks, and shared evaluation errors can make observations dependent. Recording versions, exclusions, and failures helps later investigators judge how much independent evidence the record contains.

Feasibility includes power to detect a consequential difference, the cost of supervision, privacy protection, and what action the result can change. A spending limit controls expenditure; it does not by itself establish adequate information or net social value.

The resulting report should connect methods, uncertainty, costs, and decisions. Negative and missing results need to remain visible. A successful evaluation can favor an existing arrangement, recommend a narrower change, or show that further investigation is not worth its cost.

## The permissions behind the experiment

The design still needs an institution capable of carrying it out. Requests may be controlled by the buyer, service interfaces by suppliers, and verification resources by another organization. Having each ingredient somewhere does not mean the usable experiment has been assembled.

Kenneth Arrow's 1963 study of medical care supplies an earlier institutional argument. The patient, in his account, cannot inspect the service fully before consuming it and depends on knowledge the physician possesses. Expectations that the doctor should put the patient's welfare first help organize this unequal relationship. Arrow interpreted medical institutions partly as responses to uncertainty about illness, treatment, and information. He also warned that an attempted remedy can introduce other obstacles. Institutions deserve investigation as arrangements for managing uncertainty, with consequences that require appraisal.[^chapter_12-c12-arrow1963]

James Buchanan and Yong Yoon analyze an anticommons: multiple rights to exclude can leave a resource underused. Their parking example requires complementary permits from separate holders.[^chapter_12-c12-10] Information can face a related institutional problem when useful records, interpretation, and permission to evaluate are controlled separately.

Contracting can sometimes assemble those complements. Commitment, verifiable delivery, compensable costs, and financing determine whether a feasible agreement exists. A nominal right of access does not provide the remaining inputs for free.

This should make us wary of explaining every refusal as a plan to suppress competition. A holder may face genuine privacy, security, staff, or productive costs. The useful question is whether those costs exceed the attainable benefit, whether the beneficiary can compensate the provider, and whether the arrangement can be enforced. A refusal can be privately rational without settling the wider economic case.

It should also make us wary of assuming that a mandate supplies every missing input. A rule can require access while leaving nobody responsible for defining the outcomes or paying the evaluator. Another can fund a laboratory that lacks permission to observe the relevant tasks. A useful institutional design assembles the complements needed for the stated comparison.

Rothamsted's maintained fields and archive offer a historical reference for this less glamorous work. Treatments, measurements, material, and documentation must remain connected across time. An isolated bag of grain and a beautifully organized treatment register are different things. Their joint usefulness depends on retaining the connection between them.

Institutional alternatives include existing qualified evaluators, secure contracts, shared infrastructure, and independently verified private tests. Their costs and possible common failures should be compared before establishing a new public body.

An experiment must permit a result favoring the incumbent. An institution whose survival requires the challenger to win has compromised its evidentiary purpose.

Credibility also requires stable criteria, recorded revisions, and relevant rights of challenge. Independent review can test whether the evidence supports a conclusion; the institution acting on it remains responsible for its decision.

An existing internal test may already resolve the buyer's uncertainty. Paying for a duplicate study would then spend resources without changing the decision. A missing alternative warrants further investigation when a feasible comparison could supply useful evidence.

## When the leading arrangement fails

After a leading system fails, another team needs resources to investigate, permission to test a different approach, and freedom to report what it finds. Public computing can support that work while admission rules or restrictions on publication obstruct it. What matters is whether a workable alternative can get through those steps and into use.

A failure can be isolated by independent tests or reproduced across many services sharing a common checklist and restricted evaluation route. The number of brands says little about which situation applies. Robustness concerns the practical ability to expose the failure and develop a workable alternative.

An available rival is little help if a buyer cannot transfer the records needed to test it. A dormant supplier's records can shorten recovery when they remain usable. Existing private tests or a cheaper substitute may already provide the answer. Waiting time, permissions, expense, and lost productive capacity determine whether a new access arrangement improves on those routes.

Subsidized computing that produces unusable results still consumes money, equipment, and skilled work. Official checks can spread a common error or suppress a finding that would embarrass the sponsor. A costly application process can exclude the team that needs the resources. The value of keeping an alternative available has to exceed those costs, including displaced private experiments and slower initial development.

Faster recovery in one country could reflect better equipment, a deeper pool of engineers, or other institutions alongside the access policy. Those differences belong in the explanation. So do the applicants turned away and the findings kept from public view. A list of successful recipients leaves out the people who never reached a test.

## The evidence behind a recommendation

An economic account of a widely used service should establish its costs, performance, and reach, then examine who can challenge it. The decisive questions concern a specified contribution, an observation process, and an action the evidence might change.

A buyer predicting tomorrow's workload has a different problem from one estimating the benefit of changing suppliers. Blackwell and Dubins showed how forecasts can merge under specified assumptions. In the allocation model, such agreement can coexist with unresolved productive contribution.[^chapter_12-c12-11] Agreement about the next winner does not establish why the winner wins.

Decision value matters as well as statistical precision. Blackwell's decision perspective supplies a foundation for asking what information enables a person to do.[^chapter_12-c12-12] A precisely measured tiny difference may leave a purchase unchanged. Uncertainty about a rare, costly failure may justify a targeted test. Collection costs, incentives, and usable access determine whether that test is worth buying.

The report must also identify which alternative it studies. A shadow replay preserves underlying effects, initial conditions, and response rules while events unfold differently. Changing an access rule or a training policy changes the institution. Its effects depend on how people respond under the new arrangement.

A usable account can be brief: what was compared, how it was assigned and checked, what remains uncertain, and what decision follows. Detailed methods and authorized access to records let others challenge the answer.

## Economics should test the rules

A procurement study can follow one purchase: who was eligible, how the supplier was chosen, what was checked, and what the buyer learned. Comparing the information in the award record with the information added by performance checks gives the comparison budget a real empirical setting.

Then examine the route to another test. A buyer might retain better records, evaluate a rival on suitable tasks, or purchase an independent assessment. If an alternative cannot reach the trial, identify the missing permission or resource. A bounded change in access can reveal whether new work becomes feasible and whether it improves the decision. Private testing displaced by a public experiment belongs in the cost.

Hayek described competition as a way to discover facts not previously known or used.[^chapter_12-c12-13] That makes the institutions supplying discovery part of the economy's productive equipment. They determine whether a capable alternative reaches a test, whether findings reach the people who need them, and whether those people can act.

For AI, the missing resource could be computing, a power connection, skilled staff, or suitable tasks. A team capable of a useful evaluation need not reproduce an entire frontier training operation. The smallest workable comparison can be worth more than a nominal right to enter a market it cannot reach.

Public support must be answerable to those outcomes. A remedy that adds expense without usable evidence should change or end. A private test that already resolves the uncertainty should count. The result may favor the incumbent; an institution committed to discovery has to let that finding change its decisions too.

## Growth without a way to find out

Kauffman asked whether biospheres tend to expand the possibilities available to them as rapidly as their organization can sustain. This was a conjecture about the creation of new possibilities.[^chapter_12-c12-15] Once a possibility exists, an economy faces a further task: giving it a route to development and learning from what happens.

A service can become more capable while independent ways to test its alternatives become harder to sustain. Its owner's next investment improves the visible product. A rival without a route to customers cannot build the record that would reveal whether a different investment could do better. Growth in productive capacity and growth in the capacity to judge alternatives can come apart.

The comparison budget puts a defined part of that process into the information account. It connects chances outside the favorite to limits on what a reward history can reveal about productive input effects. The conditions in Chapter 3 identify a case where continuing that history cannot finish the attribution job. Evidence from another source can matter precisely because more of the same is insufficient.

At network scale, preventing a challenger from beginning can remove a future comparison before a conventional performance test is even available. Preserving discovery therefore means protecting feasible development routes as well as comparing finished products. A right to start that cannot be exercised leaves the established result in command of the next result.

## The record we choose to leave

At Rothamsted, someone had to preserve the samples, label them, and keep the treatment records intelligible. Later researchers could put new questions to that material. An archive that had been discarded could not have answered them.

An economy makes a similar choice whenever it allocates another opportunity. It can support the established supplier and also preserve an independent route through which someone else could demonstrate a better method. It can distribute a share of capital's returns more widely and give people resources with which to attempt something new. Or it can let control over the next attempt accumulate with control over the last reward.

The choice concerns who can produce evidence that the existing record does not supply. A potential challenger needs a workable beginning before there can be a finished product to compare.

An economy committed to independent discovery has reason to reward useful work while preserving routes to challenge it. A substantial return can finance the next invention. Giving its recipient authority over whether anyone else can attempt one adds a different power, with different consequences. The payment and the power should be judged separately.

Start with a gatekeeper controlling an indispensable route to evaluation. If closing it threatens a lasting loss of useful comparison, protecting access preserves the possibility of discovering which supplier deserves the next contract. Documented interfaces, voluntary portability, secure evaluation access, and independently reviewed terms can make an enforceable duty concrete. The obligation should follow the actual obstruction, with privacy, security, resource costs, and investment included in its design. When useful private evidence already answers the affected decision, duplicating the experiment may buy nothing.

Act while the alternative can still develop. A team cannot be required to demonstrate years of learning before obtaining the customers through whom that learning would occur. Before closure becomes difficult to reverse, a credible attempt needs a route to its first test. Keeping that route open lets customers judge the result without promising the entrant a contract or permanent support.

Where restrictive conduct prevents valuable learning and imposes a cost on others, leaving that cost outside the decision rewards too much restriction. An access duty may prevent the loss. A corrective charge may address a remaining external harm. Its justification depends on the missing comparison, the decisions affected, and what feasible replacement would achieve. A large profit supplies neither a damage meter nor a reason to ignore a demonstrated obstruction.

Opening a gate accomplishes little for someone unable to afford the attempt. Independent learning can benefit people beyond the buyer paying for it, leaving that buyer too little reason to meet its whole cost.[^chapter_12-c12-16] Financing shared capabilities spreads the bill for discovery and gives more people the means to participate. Capital income and inheritance taxation can support a shared computing facility, a first demonstration, or broader ownership of productive assets. The case rests on the opportunities those arrangements create. Investment effects, avoidance, administration, and the benefits delivered determine workable terms. An exact retrospective division of each fortune into merit and position is unnecessary for choosing them.

The capacity to begin something different should remain a shared opportunity. When private systems close practical routes to earning, building, and owning, protecting the owners' investment should carry a reciprocal obligation to share the returns. Everyone living under that arrangement should retain a stake in the prosperity it produces. Membership in society should establish that basic claim before an employer, investor, or evaluator judges what a person might be worth.

A universal social dividend makes that stake independent of a gatekeeper's approval. The worker who never receives a first assignment cannot show the skills that experience might have developed. The founder who never reaches a customer cannot show the business those sales might have sustained. Their payment should not depend on reconstructing those missing careers. It should extend equally to someone who chooses to care for a relative, study, or never run a company. People should share in prosperity without having to win admission to someone else's plan for it.

The payment should be regular, unconditional, and universal; the tax contributions financing it should be progressive. Returns on broadly held productive assets should help sustain it. An adequate income floor requires durable funding, useful public services, and investment in the production on which the payment depends.

Ownership and entry require attention together. A dividend secures an income claim; workable access preserves a chance to challenge the arrangement producing it. Public computing can also become a gate if one official evaluator controls admission. Shared ownership, independent evaluation, published project-eligibility rules, and appeal should operate together. Government should be as open to a challenge to its preferred answer as a private gatekeeper is required to be.

MusicLab gave the same recordings more than one opportunity to develop a following. Lockdown finished first in one world and fortieth in another.[^chapter_12-c12-14] Its success was real in one history. Its neglect was real in the other. Neither chart could tell the whole story by itself.

We will rarely have both charts for an economy. We still have to choose how its rewards are taxed, which rights accompany ownership, and what opportunities remain available to people whose work has not yet acquired a record. The choice can rest on shared aims and practical evidence about institutions. It need not rest on a claim that today's distribution has finally established who deserves everything they received.

A successful economy needs reasons to keep producing and the means for other people to begin. Protecting investment, financing useful challenges, and limiting the power to close an essential gate serve those purposes together. Success can earn a reward. The next opportunity must remain open to a different answer.

---

<a id="epilogue"></a>

# Epilogue

## The next opportunity

At Rothamsted in England, wheat grows in an experiment begun in 1843. Across its plots, researchers have compared mineral fertilizers, manure, and no fertilizer. They have kept records and saved samples of crops and soil. Generations of researchers have inherited both the harvests and the differences that help explain them.[^epilogue-e-3]

Give every plot the same treatment, and the field might produce more wheat. It would also stop producing that continuing comparison. An untreated plot can look like wasted ground when this year's crop is all that counts. Its value changes when we count what it helps people learn.

Someone has to keep making room for it. Soil must be tended, samples preserved, records understood. The experiment has changed as agricultural methods and scientific questions have changed. The knowledge available to later researchers depends on work that earlier generations chose to do.

After the olive-oil business failed, I began putting more of my attention into local production, networks, and relationships. I wanted to build from opportunities close enough to act on, with people I could come to know. Years spent imagining a better past had made the next small beginning feel more valuable.

I still think about the paths I did not take. But another life cannot answer an email, make a delivery, or help a neighbor begin something. Those things have to happen in this one.

A market leaves a different kind of field behind it. Orders, wages, investments, and download counts record where activity went. They can tell us a great deal about the businesses and people who received those opportunities. The rejected application usually leaves a thinner file.

For a beginner, an assignment can bring pay, practice, a reference, and something to show the next employer. Turn someone down for lack of experience, and the decision can help perpetuate the very lack it cites. Another year of work by the experienced applicant will not reveal what the beginner would have learned. To find that out, someone has to offer a beginning.

The same difficulty can arise when software does the work. A company might send thousands of tasks to AI agents using one model and build an impressive record of completed jobs. To discover whether another system would perform better, it needs suitable tasks, access to that system, and a way to compare results. Another million completions by the incumbent do not automatically provide that comparison.

A rival listed on a website has permission to exist. Getting its work into an evaluation, its product in front of customers, or its findings into a decision is a further step. When a gatekeeper controls those routes, it can help decide which alternatives acquire evidence and which remain promises. That is where epistemic monopoly reaches into everyday economic life.

We can spend heavily on producing answers while spending little on discovering whether we are asking the right system. We can reward experience while closing the routes through which someone else could acquire it. A busy economy can keep repeating a choice without getting much better at explaining it.

The next AI company should have a workable route to demonstrate that it can do better. A worker rejected by one platform should have somewhere else to establish what they can do. A researcher should be able to investigate a dominant system without giving its owner authority over the findings.

If the only route to a credible test belongs to the company being challenged, who gets to decide whether the challenge is heard? Leaving that decision entirely with the owner gives it authority over the evidence of its own superiority. An enforceable access duty can keep a useful route open. The rival may need a secure interface, the worker an independent assessment, the researcher access to a credible test. Those arrangements should preserve privacy, safety, and useful investment while making the challenge possible. Their costs belong in the decision, alongside the value of finding out.

We should act while an alternative still has a route to development. Asking for a finished rival after closing the means of building one makes success a condition for obtaining the opportunity to succeed. A credible attempt should be able to reach a test before its absence becomes another reason to dismiss it.

A team still needs resources after the gate opens. Capital income and inheritance taxation can help finance independent experimentation and broaden ownership of productive assets. A shared computing facility can let that team attempt a different design. A grant can pay for a first demonstration. A share in productive capital can let more people benefit from the work machines perform. Funding should follow usable opportunities and results, with costs, investment effects, and displaced private efforts counted. An economy that relies on discovery has a reason to help pay for the means of making it happen.

When a restriction prevents valuable learning, its author should face the cost imposed on others. Opening the route may accomplish more than collecting money after closing it. Financing common capabilities need not wait for the treasury to measure the unearned portion of every fortune.

A universal basic income should give everyone a regular stake in the prosperity these systems produce. Someone whose opportunity never reached a trial should not have to prove the career they might have had before sharing in the returns. The payment should be unconditional. The routes to work, ownership, and invention should remain open as well.

Public institutions must remain open to challenge themselves. A laboratory should be able to publish an inconvenient finding. A team refused access should have a meaningful appeal. Money for discovery should leave room for different methods, and for evidence that the established supplier remains best. The purpose is to let what happens in the test change what happens afterward.

At MusicLab, that possibility was built into the experiment. The same forty-eight recordings entered separate worlds. Listeners made choices, those choices became visible to later listeners, and the charts developed differently. Lockdown finished first in one world and fortieth in another.[^epilogue-e-2]

Imagine having only the first chart. We could play the winning recording, examine its qualities, and write a persuasive account of why it deserved to lead. With only the other chart, we could do the same for its failure. The second history makes both explanations harder to deliver with such confidence.

We can admire an achievement without treating its reward as the final word on everyone else's ability. The applicant, the business, or the idea with a thin record may have needed an opportunity before there could be much to record. A society that wants to discover what people can do has to make some of those beginnings possible.

Most shadow futures will leave no second chart. We cannot recover every missed opportunity or run every possible experiment. We can choose rules that reward useful investment, finance credible alternatives, and prevent an essential gateway from becoming its owner's permanent power over other people's beginnings. Agreement about those rules can guide us even when agreement about each person's exact contribution remains beyond our evidence.

Lockdown's other chart existed because someone made room for another history. The recording was ready. The opportunity had to be built.

An economy should reward its winners without giving them ownership of everyone else's chance.

<a id="acknowledgments"></a>

# Acknowledgments

This book depends on researchers who made their experiments, methods, working papers and findings available for others to examine, and on the people who preserve public records and scientific archives. The notes and references identify the work behind its arguments and examples. Particular debts run to the MusicLab researchers and to the long traditions of information economics, experimental design, reinforcement, discovery and public finance discussed throughout the book.

The comparison-budget argument, its interpretation, and its proposed applications are my responsibility.
